## Overview

In the Dharampura quarter of Lahore, once the garden-suburb of Baghbanpura (or Hashimpura), stands the shrine of Hazrat Mian Mir — Mir Mohammad of Sind — one of the greatest Sufi saints of the Mughal age and among the most beloved holy figures in the history of the city. A master of the Qadiri order, Mian Mir was revered in his own lifetime by beggars and emperors alike for the fierceness of his asceticism, the depth of his learning, and the breadth of a heart that welcomed Muslim, Sikh, Hindu, and Christian without distinction. For nearly four centuries his red-sandstone tomb has drawn pilgrims seeking peace, healing, and blessing, and because of his celebrated friendship with the Sikh Gurus it endures as one of Lahore’s living symbols of interfaith reverence. To understand the shrine is first to understand the life of the man buried within it, and so this account begins with the saint himself before turning to the tomb, its miracles, and the devotion that surrounds it.

## The Life of the Saint

Mian Mir was born in the sixteenth century in Sehwan (Sivastan) in Sind — the town lying, as the prince Dara Shikoh later noted, between the cities of Thatta and Bhakkar — into a devout and learned family. His father, Qazi Sain Datta, was a man of religious standing respected throughout Sind, and the family traced its lineage to the second Caliph of Islam, Umar al-Farooq, from which descent Mian Mir is often given the epithet “Farooqi.” His father died while he was still a child, and the decisive turn of his life came early: at about the age of twelve, with the consent and blessing of his widowed mother, the boy left home in search of inner enlightenment at the feet of some God-oriented soul, and for a time he wandered as a seeker. He was drawn to the inward path and was initiated into the Qadiri silsila — the order that traces its spiritual descent to Shaikh Abd al-Qadir Jilani of Baghdad — and from his shaykh he received training and, in time, the khilafat, the authority to guide others.

Around the age of twenty-five, with his teacher’s permission, Mian Mir came to Lahore and settled in the leafy neighbourhood of Baghbanpura on the edge of the city, beside the river Ravi. There he would remain for the rest of a very long life — by tradition some eighty-eight years — passing his days and nights in devotion, teaching, and the disciplined practice of the spiritual arts, until Lahore itself became inseparable from his name. He died in the city in 1635 (1045 AH). In his final illness, worn by age and by the gout that had crippled his legs, he is said to have refused treatment, explaining that he did not wish to be dulled into unconsciousness: he wished to meet death fully aware, and to feel it come. He had spent some sixty years in the same small cell, and it was there that he passed away.

## The Ascetic and His Way

Mian Mir’s contemporaries remembered him above all for the severity and the sincerity of his renunciation. In his hujra — the bare cell in which he lived — he spread nothing more than a piece of old sackcloth upon the floor, and upon that humble seat even the emperors of Hindustan did not hesitate to sit. He wore only coarse homespun khaddar, washed his own clothes with his own hands in the waters of the Ravi, and carried none of the outward marks of a Sufi master: no staff to lean upon in his age, and no patched cloak (khirqa) to advertise his station. Once, when a devotee brought him a staff and he had walked a few steps leaning upon it, he cast it away, saying, “He who has Allah for his support has no need of any artificial prop.” When the wealthy pressed gifts of money and goods upon him for his needs, he turned them away: “I stand in no need of these. I am not a beggar; I am rich, for my Provider looks after my needs — give them to the poor and the needy.” A dervish who begs from another beggar, he held, forfeits his trust in God.

He was a renowned adept of habs-e-dam, the disciplined regulation of the breath — a practice akin to the yogic pranayama — which he would carry through the night from the Isha prayer until the light of dawn, a discipline he passed on to his own successor. Though a man of deep and illumined learning who could expound the intricacies of the Qur’an and Hadith to the astonishment of the scholars of his day, he neither wrote prose nor composed verse, and showed no wish to keep letters or set down his own words; he held that when a dervish scatters his inner state upon paper the secret entrusted to him by God is unveiled to those unworthy to keep it. Ordinarily he was not eager even for visitors: he would welcome a guest, raise his hands in prayer and blessing for him, and then gently ask him to return to his own affairs.

## His Teaching

Mian Mir’s teaching, as his disciples preserved it, was as demanding as his life. He taught that a man is a composite of three things — _nafs_ (the ego), _dil_ (the heart), and _rooh_ (the soul) — and that the whole labour of the spiritual traveller is to bring these into equilibrium: to submit to the _Sharia_, to walk the path of _tariqat_ in search of _marifat_, and so to reach _haqiqat_, the Truth. He counselled his followers to fix the heart on God alone and to empty it of the love of the world; to live plainly, eating little, speaking little, and sleeping little; to meet honour and insult with the same evenness of soul; and to accept all weal and woe alike as a blessing from God. Above all he treated every human being, of whatever faith, as a creature of the one God — and he did not flatter power: he would, unmindful of royal displeasure, seek to dissuade the emperor and his sons from any act he judged unjust or unethical. In an age of magnificence, he made poverty and truthfulness his splendour.

## A Saint Who Wrote No Verse

Uniquely among the great figures of Punjabi Sufism, Mian Mir left behind no _diwan_ of poetry — a silence that was itself a kind of teaching. Yet verse gathered around him. Tradition records that when his disciples spoke of raising a fitting monument over his resting place, he forbade any dome or mausoleum, and is said to have uttered a Persian couplet on the vanity of such display:

صورتِ قبرم ز بعدِ مرگ ویرانہ خوشتر است
نیستی مانندِ من با خاک یکساں خوشتر است

[“The face of my grave, after death, is better left in ruins; for one like me, to be as nothing, level with the dust, is better.”]

His most devoted disciple at court, the Mughal prince Dara Shikoh, honoured him in Persian verse as the very pole of sainthood of the age — Mir Mohammad, he wrote, is the pathfinder for all the friends of God of this age; while the Qadiri line endures it shall lead others, so long as the universe abides. That a prince of the empire should write so of a barefoot dervish in sackcloth is itself a measure of the awe that Mian Mir commanded.

## Mian Mir and the Mughal Court

For all his withdrawal from the world, Mian Mir’s fame reached the very summit of the Mughal state. The Emperor Jahangir sought him out; Dara Shikoh records a long interview between the emperor and the saint, and relates that Mian Mir — “notwithstanding his great age and weakness” — took the trouble to come to him, and that the emperor, deeply moved, wrote to him both before and after their meeting, requesting to see him again. Such visitors the saint received seated on his sackcloth, offered a prayer for them, and then dismissed with courtesy to their own concerns.

His deepest imprint on the age, however, came through his disciples. His foremost successor was Mullah Shah Badakhshi, who carried on the master’s nightly practice of habs-e-dam and became the spiritual guide of Prince Dara Shikoh and, through him, of the emperor Shah Jahan’s brilliant daughter, the princess Jahanara. It was Dara Shikoh — the crown prince famed for seeking the common ground between Islam and the mystical traditions of India — who became Mian Mir’s most ardent admirer and who composed, in honour of the saint and his circle, the Persian hagiography _Sakīnat al-Auliyā_ (“The Tranquillity of the Saints”), today one of the principal sources for the saint’s life. The nearness of so many of the imperial family to Mian Mir’s circle carried, in the end, a tragic weight: when Dara Shikoh was defeated and put to death by his brother Aurangzeb in the war of succession, the prince and his wife Nadira Begum were laid to rest close to Mian Mir, and tradition holds that Aurangzeb drew upon stone from the complex in the raising of the great Badshahi Mosque of Lahore.

## The Mystical Court: Dara Shikoh and Jahanara

The circle that formed around Mian Mir and his successor Mullah Shah became, for one brief and luminous generation, the spiritual heart of the Mughal court. Prince Dara Shikoh, drawn through Mullah Shah into the Qadiri way, gave his life to the search for the unity underlying Islam and the mystical traditions of India. He gathered the lives and sayings of the saints in his _Safīnat al-Auliyā_ and _Sakīnat al-Auliyā_; he wrote the _Majma-ul-Bahrain_ — “The Mingling of the Two Oceans” — to show the deep harmony between Sufi and Indian metaphysics; and he caused the Upanishads to be rendered into Persian, a labour that would one day carry Indian thought to Europe. His sister, the princess Jahanara — Shah Jahan’s best-loved daughter — was herself a disciple of Mullah Shah and set down her own spiritual journey in the _Risala-i-Sahibiya_, one of the rare mystical works to come from the hand of a woman of the imperial house. Through these royal disciples the bare Qadiri cell beside the Ravi reached into the very centre of the empire.

Yet the same nearness marked the circle for tragedy. When the throne passed, in blood, to the austere and orthodox Aurangzeb rather than to his mystically minded elder brother, Dara Shikoh was condemned as a heretic and put to death in 1659. The liberal, seeking, syncretic spirit that Mian Mir and Mullah Shah had nourished was driven from the court — to survive thereafter not in palaces but at shrines such as Mian Mir’s, where it had first been kindled. That the prince and his wife were buried beside the saint they revered is a fitting close to the story: in death, the seeker of the union of faiths returned to the threshold of the master who had taught him that God’s door is open to all.

## The Golden Temple and a Friendship Across Faiths

The most famous tradition attached to Mian Mir binds his memory to the Sikh faith. He was a close friend of the fifth Sikh Guru, Arjan Dev, and of the sixth, Guru Hargobind, and by long-standing Sikh tradition it was Mian Mir whom Guru Arjan Dev invited to lay the foundation stone of the Harmandir Sahib — the Golden Temple — at Amritsar, so that the holiest shrine of the Sikhs might be founded by the hand of a Muslim saint. Later historians have weighed the episode carefully: the contemporary Persian chronicles do not mention it, and scholars such as Madanjit Kaur, in _The Golden Temple: Past and Present_, note the absence of early documentary proof, while others have argued that the political tensions of the age — the involvement of the Sirhindi court faction in the death of Guru Arjan Dev — would have made such a gesture difficult. Yet the objection does not really hold, for Mian Mir’s cordial and fraternal relations with the fifth Guru are well attested, and he belonged not to the Sirhindi (Naqshbandi) circle but to the Qadiri order. Whatever the historian concludes about the foundation stone itself, the tradition is cherished across communities as a luminous emblem of the friendship between Muslims and Sikhs in Punjab, and it continues to draw Sikh pilgrims to the saint’s memory. What all the sources agree upon is the temper of the man: Mian Mir received Sikhs, Hindus, and Christians with the same courtesy and reverence he showed his fellow Muslims, and treated them all as children of the one God.

## A Thread in Lahore’s Sufi Heritage

Mian Mir’s life also wove him into the deeper Sufi history of Lahore. He is said to have kept a forty-day retreat (chilla) at the khanqah of Data Ganj Bakhsh, Ali Hujwiri — the eleventh-century saint whose _Kashf al-Mahjub_ is among the earliest Persian treatises on Sufism, and whose shrine, Data Darbar, is the spiritual heart of the city. In going to sit in devotion at Data Sahib’s threshold, Mian Mir joined his own sanctity to that of Lahore’s patron saint, and later devotion has treasured the link as binding together the two greatest Sufi presences of the city across the span of five centuries.

## Miracles and Folklore

As with every great saint, a rich body of miracle-tales (karāmāt) gathered around Mian Mir, and they were recorded and retold well into the colonial era, when British collectors of Punjabi folklore took them down. Best known is the story of the dove: a hunter’s stone struck down a bird that had been sweetly praising God, and at Mian Mir’s touch it revived and flew away unharmed; when the hunter aimed once more, the stone turned back and wounded his own hand, until, repenting and vowing never again to harm a winged creature, he was healed. Devotees tell, too, of a black snake that came to the saint as he sat by the Ravi, circled him three times, and slipped quietly away; of a mute boy whom he taught to utter “Bismillah,” after which the child could speak; of a handkerchief he gave that healed the sick who used it in faith; and of food that appeared, in answer to his prayer, when there was nothing in the house to set before a guest.

Many such tales turn on his knowledge of hidden things and his power over sickness and death. A man came seeking his blessing for a journey to Khorasan; Mian Mir advised him not to go, for the way, he said, was closed — and so, unseen, it proved. Another came in anguish over a son choking and suffocating; the saint fell into a trance, called for a cup of water, breathed his recitation over it, and by it the child was relieved. To a certain visitor he said simply, “you may depart” — and within a few days the man breathed his last, the words proving true. His disciples cherished their own intimate wonders: Meerak Mohammad Lahori relates how, having taken up and chewed a discarded betel-leaf from the saint’s own mouth, he was carried away into so deep an ecstasy that his breast seemed near to bursting with felicity. Stories such as these, more than any doctrine, are what bound ordinary people to the saint, and they still draw the troubled and the hopeful to his shrine.

## Death and the Building of the Shrine

Mian Mir died in 1635, and though he had expressly forbidden any tomb over his grave, a mausoleum of red sandstone was raised at his resting place in Baghbanpura in the years that followed, during the lifetime of Dara Shikoh, who could not bear that his master should lie unmarked. It grew into a dignified monument in the Mughal manner, its chambers finished in fine stone, and around it, in time, were gathered the graves of others of the imperial circle who had loved him — Dara Shikoh himself, executed in 1659, and his wife Nadira Begum among them. Thus the barefoot dervish who owned nothing and had asked for nothing came to rest at the centre of a small city of tombs, his wish overruled by the devotion of a prince.

## The Shrine Through the Centuries

The tomb passed through the turbulence of later ages. In the Sikh period much of its valuable stone was carried off under the builders of Maharaja Ranjit Singh, who prized the material, and — by tradition — stone from the wider complex had earlier been drawn upon for imperial building under Aurangzeb; yet through every change of rule the shrine endured as a place of devotion. Under British rule it came under the care of the department of ancient monuments, and in 1985 it was formally declared a protected site by the Punjab Auqaf Department, which administers it to this day together with the shrine’s traditional sajjada-nashins, the hereditary custodians. The mausoleum stands in the Dharampura (Hashimpura) area of Lahore, its dome and gateway a familiar landmark, with an attached mosque and open courtyards to receive the crowds who gather there. Successive generations have repaired and adorned it, so that the austere saint who wished only for a ruined and level grave is honoured, instead, by one of the enduring monuments of the city.

## Devotion Today

The shrine of Mian Mir remains a living centre of popular piety. On ordinary days it draws on the order of one to three thousand visitors, who come to recite the Fatiha, tie vows, and seek the saint’s intercession for healing, for children, and for relief from hardship; many speak of the peace they feel within its walls and of prayers answered there. Qawwali is sung on Thursday evenings, the five daily prayers are held in the shrine mosque, and langar — free food — is distributed to visitors, sometimes twice a day. The shrine’s great annual occasion is the urs, held on 7 February, which marks the saint’s union with the Divine; the courtyards then fill with hundreds of thousands of devotees for days of qawwali, prayer, and communal feasting. Across the centuries — under Mughal, Sikh, and British rule, and in the Pakistan of today — the shrine has served as a shared space of devotion, remembered by writers from Dara Shikoh to Khushwant Singh as a place where the boundaries of community grew soft.

## Legacy

Mian Mir stands in memory as the very type of the Sufi ideal: a man who owned nothing and was sought by emperors; who wrote not a single verse and yet was praised in verse by a prince; who, rooted absolutely in his own faith, made his threshold a meeting-place for people of every religion. His disciples shaped the spiritual life of the Mughal court; his friendship with the Sikh Gurus became a legend of tolerance that Punjab still cherishes; his forty-day retreat at Data Darbar bound him to the patron saint of Lahore; and his tomb remains one of the anchors of the city’s sacred landscape. In honouring Mian Mir, Lahore remembers not only a saint of extraordinary austerity and learning, but a vision of holiness generous enough to hold an entire, plural world.

## Bibliography

- Gyani Brahma Singh ‘Brahma’, _Hazrat Mian Mir and the Sufi Tradition_ (Patiala: Publication Bureau, Punjabi University, 1994).
- Muhammad Dara Shikoh, _Sakīnat al-Auliyā_ (Persian; the prince’s hagiography of Mian Mir and his circle).
- Komal Butter, “Emotions and Miracles: Folktales of the Mian Mir Shrine in Nineteenth-Century Lahore,” _Journal of the Punjab University Historical Society_ 38:2 (2025).
- Madanjit Kaur, _The Golden Temple: Past and Present_ (referenced on the foundation-stone tradition).
- Shrines Project field survey, Mian Mir responses (surveyor: Muhammad Rizwan), 2026.

## From Mufti Ghulam Sarwar Lahori, Hadiqat al-Awliya

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/hadeeqat_ul_aulia.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

**What the row gains.** A full entry (no. 21, pp. 60–62, folios 48–50) and seven further
appearances: the book's given name Shaikh Muhammad Mir and the epithet **بالا پیر** the row does not
carry; both parents named; a rauza built by Dara Shikoh and stripped by Ranjit Singh; a named
khalifa (Mulla Shah) heading a Qadiri chain that runs to 1213 AH; a locality and a Lahore road
called after him; and a rival order's hostile anecdote about his line. Against that, the editor's
own table locates the khanqah at Miani Sahib, where the archive row says Alam Ganj / Dharampura.

**Saint**
- Entry no. 21, "شیخ محمد میر المشہور بہ میاں میر قادری لاہوری", listed in the contents at printed
  p. 48 (p. 15, folio 3) and printed at pp. 60–62, folios 48–50. The persons index gives eight
  printed pages: "میاں میر لاہوری | ۴۸ ، ۴۹ ، ۵۰ ، ۵۳ ، ۵۴ ، ۵۸ ، ۶۶ ، ۷۰" (p. 331, folio 319) →
  PDF pp. 60, 61, 62, 65, 66, 70, 78, 82. Principal treatment at folios 48–54.
- **Personal name.** The index asserts it by cross-reference: "میر محمد | دیکھئے میاں میر" — Mir
  Muhammad, see Mian Mir (p. 329, folio 317), matching the archive's "Mir Mohammad of Sind". A
  second, separate and uncross-referenced entry "میر محمد | ۱۲۱" stands two lines above the
  see-reference's target (p. 331, folio 319); whether it is the same man is not stated. The editor
  glosses the titles: he was called Mir Muhammad, with the titles Mian Mir and Shah Mir,
  "میاں مقامی زبان میں صاحب کے معنوں میں" — *Mian* meaning "sahib" in the local language, used for
  respect (Sakinat al-Auliya 25) (p. 61, folio 49, حاشیہ).
- **The epithet بالا پیر** is the book's usual form for him outside his own entry (pp. 69, 78, 222,
  242, 254–255, 288; also "میاں میر بالاپیر", p. 242). The bare "میاں میر" is also used (pp. 267,
  270). Neither form is in the archive row.
- **Do not merge the two بالا پیرs.** Entry no. 199 names "سید محمد بالا پیر گیلانی", a grandfather
  of Miran Muhammad Shah's wife, and entry no. 200 names "میاں میر بالا پیر لاہوری" (both p. 254,
  folio 242). These are different men in the book's own telling; chunk 023's "میاں میر بالاپیر" is
  the second.
- **Parentage and birthplace**: father Qazi Sain Ditta b. Qazi Qalandar Faruqi, mother Bibi Fatima
  bint Qazi Qadan; "سیوستان میں آپ پیدا ہوئے", he was born at Sewistan; his father died when he was
  seven (p. 60, folio 48). The mother is indexed as
  "فاطمہ بی بی (والدہ میاں میر) | ۴۸" (p. 325, folio 313); note three other Bibi Fatimas stand in
  the same ف run (p. 325).
- **Birth year — author and editor disagree.** The author, on the authority of the author of
  *Safinat al-Auliya*, gives birth "سال نوسو ساٹھ" (960), death 1045 AH, age eighty-seven, arrival
  at Lahore at twenty-five and sixty years' residence (pp. 61–62, folios 49–50, chunk 005). The
  editor dates the birth to **957 AH / 1550 CE** on Dara Shikoh's authority (Sakinat al-Auliya 95)
  (p. 61, folio 49, حاشیہ, chunk 005). The death year 1045 AH is not contradicted anywhere in this
  group's material.
- **Initiation from his own mother.** At twelve he finished his religious education and
  "اپنی والدہ ماجدہ کی خدمت میں بسلسلہ عالیہ قادریہ بیعت کر کے" took bai'at in the Qadiri silsila at
  his mother's hand, and only later, **with her permission**, went to Shaikh Khizr Sistani for
  completion; after the khirqa of khilafat his pir directed him to Lahore, where he settled (p. 61,
  folio 49). He is the khalifa of Shaikh Khizr Sistani, with the full chain of murids given back
  through Qutb al-Afaq Sayyid Abdul Razzaq to his father Abdul Qadir Gilani (p. 60, folio 48).
- **Education at Lahore**: the current sciences with Maulana Saad Allah Lahori and Akhund Nemat
  Allah (p. 61, folio 49, حاشیہ) — the same Saad Allah the editor names as Shah Husain's teacher
  (p. 56, folio 44, chunk 004).
- **Austerity**: he never slept at night; his حبس دم [OCR?] was such that a whole night passed in a
  single breath; he broke his fast only after a week; and sometimes in absorption a month at a time
  passed without his eating (p. 61, folio 49).
- **Siblings**: four more brothers — Mian Qazi, Qazi Usman, Qazi Tahir, Qazi Muhammad — and two
  sisters, Bibi Badi and **Bibi Jamal Khatun**, the latter having her own entry in the chapter on
  righteous women (contents no. 200, printed p. 242) (p. 62, folio 50).
- **The sister's entry (no. 200, pp. 254–255, folios 242–243)** is the richest family material:
  "بی بی جمال خاتون … ترک و تجرید میں رابعہ وقت۔ نسبت عالیہ سلسلہ قادریہ اس کو اپنے برادر حقیقی
  میاں میر بالا پیر لاہوری اور والدہ ماجدہ سے حاصل تھی" — the Qadiri nisbat reached her from her
  full brother **and from their mother** (p. 254, folio 242). "کتاب سکینۃ الاولیاء و سفینۃ الاولیاء
  میں حضرت دارا شکوہ نے اس بی بی کا مفصل حال لکھا ہے" — Dara Shikoh wrote her life in detail in both
  books (p. 254, folio 242).
- **Her death and burial, contradicted by the editor, twice over.** Author: "وفات بی بی کی سال ایک
  ہزار ہجری میں ہوئی اور مدفن شریف شہر لاہور میں [illegible] خانقاہ کے اندر واقع ہے" — d. 1000 AH,
  buried inside a [illegible] khanqah in the city of Lahore (p. 255, folio 243, chunk 024). Editor:
  "مفتی صاحب کا مندرجہ سال وفات ۱۰۰۰ ھ درست نہیں ہے۔ بلکہ دارا شکوہ نے ۱۰۵۷ ھ تحریر کیا ہے",
  quoting "سُن شریف بی بی جمال خاتون از شصت متجاوز است و در سیوستان موطن خود اقامت دارند و ہرگز از
  آں جا بجائے دیگر نرفتہ اند ........ وفات ایشاں در روز سہ شنبہ بیست و ہفتم ماہ ربیع الاول سال ہزار
  و پنجاہ و ہفت واقع شدہ است (سکینۃ الاولیا ص ۱۳۱)" (p. 255, folio 243, حاشیہ 1, chunk 024). The
  quotation contradicts the **burial** as well as the year: a woman who never left Sehwan cannot be
  buried in a Lahore khanqah.

**Shrine / Darbar**
- "روضہ حضرت کا لاہور میں زیارت گاہ خلق اللہ ہے" — his rauza in Lahore is a place of pilgrimage for
  God's creatures (p. 62, folio 50), followed by a Persian chronogram quatrain,
  "میر دنیا و دیں میاں میر ست…".
- **Built by Dara Shikoh, stripped by Ranjit Singh**: "ان کا روضہ دارا شکوہ نے کئی لاکھ روپے خرچ کر
  کے بنوایا تھا جس کے پتھر تمام و کمال رنجیت سنگھ اتروا کر امرت سر لے گیا" — Dara Shikoh had the
  rauza built at an expense of several lakh rupees, and Ranjit Singh had its stones entirely taken
  down and carried off to Amritsar (p. 69, folio 57); the editor sources the point to Kanhiya Lal's
  *Tarikh-e Lahore* 245 (p. 69, folio 57, حاشیہ 3). *Note the sentence's subject: it stands in Mulla
  Shah Badakhshi's entry (no. 29) and "ان کا روضہ" reads as Mulla Shah's; the chunk placed it under
  this row. A reader of the page must settle whose rauza was stripped before this enters the entry.*
- **A settlement inside a shrine precinct**: Mulla Shah's "مزار گوہر بار لاہور میں بیرون مزار میاں
  میر بالا پیر ہے", outside Mian Mir Bala Pir's mazar, and "اب ان کے مزار کے احاطہ میں موضع میاں میر
  آباد ہے" — the village **Mian Mir Abad** now stands within the enclosure of his mazar (p. 69,
  folio 57), recorded by the author as a present fact.
- **The appendix sites three graves by reference to his** (pp. 287–288, folios 275–276): row 9,
  "شیخ محمد میر المشہور بہ میاں میر قادری لاہوری | لاہور | میاں میر نزد [illegible] ریلوے سٹیشن";
  row 12, Khwaja Bihari "متصل روضہ میاں میر", present location "قصبہ میاں میر۔ بالمقابل مقبرہ حضرت
  میاں میر"; row 15, Mulla Shah Qadiri "بیرون مزار میاں میر بالا پیر", present location
  "اندرون موضع میاں میر". The locality itself is thus called **قصبہ / موضع میاں میر**. The word after
  "نزد" in row 9 is `[illegible]`.
- Khwaja Bihari's mazar is "متصل روضہ میاں میر رح کے", adjoining Mian Mir's rauza (p. 66, folio 54).
- **A walled khanqah enclosure containing another saint's grave, and a rival address.** The editor's
  table, row ۷۵: "بی بی جمال خاتون قدس سرہ | اندر احاطہ خانقاہ حضرت میاں میر | کراچی ریلوے لائن اور
  میانی صاحب گاؤں کے درمیان" — 1875 address inside the احاطہ of Mian Mir's khanqah; present location
  between the Karachi railway line and Miani Sahib village (p. 291, folio 279, chunk 028). **Set
  against the appendix's own rows** (chunk 027), which put the site at قصبہ میاں میر near a railway
  station (pp. 287–288); the two location columns do not obviously describe one place, and the
  archive row reads Alam Ganj / Dharampura. Three readings, none reconciled.
- **A Lahore road named for the shrine, in the author's present.** Sayyid Mahmud Huzuri's tomb lies
  "لاہور کے باہر میاں میر کی سڑک کے پاس", outside Lahore near Mian Mir's road (p. 44, folio 32); Pir
  Hadi Rahnuma's maqbara stands "لاہور کے باہر لب سڑک جو میاں میر کو انارکلی سے جاتی ہے", on the edge
  of the road that runs from Anarkali to Mian Mir (p. 267, folio 255); and the place index carries
  "سرک [OCR?] میاں میر | ۳۲" (p. 334, folio 322) → PDF p. 44, the first word flagged.

**Practices & Events**
- **An overnight stay and a dream-initiation at the shrine**, quoted by the author from Shah
  Muhammad Ghaus's own *Risala-e Ghausia*: "جب میں تلاش حق لاہور میں پہنچا۔ رات کو مقبرہ عالیہ میاں
  میر میں قیام پذیر ہوا۔ خواب میں میاں میر بالا پیر مجھ پر ظاہر ہوئے۔ اور بکمال توجہ ایک شغل عنایت
  کیا۔ صبح کے وقت وہاں سے اٹھ کر شیخ حامد لاہوری کی خدمت میں آیا اور کسی شغل کے واسطے ان سے بھی سوال
  کیا۔ وہ ہنسے اور فرمایا تجھ کو وہی شغل کافی ہے جو آج رات میاں میر بالا پیر سے عطا ہوا ہے" — "When I
  reached Lahore in search of the truth, I lodged for the night at the exalted maqbara of Mian Mir.
  In a dream Mian Mir Bala Pir appeared to me and with complete attention bestowed a *shughl* on me.
  In the morning I rose from there and came to Shaikh Hamid Lahori and asked him too for some
  shughl. He laughed and said: the very shughl bestowed on you last night by Mian Mir Bala Pir is
  enough for you." (p. 78, folio 66 [marker: 76]).
- **A quantified khanqah grain economy**: "حضرت کے گھر میں قریب دو من کے غلہ گندم تھا۔ ایک دن بی بی
  نے اس میں ہاتھ ڈالا تو یہ برکت ہوئی کہ ایک سال تک برابر روزمرہ غلہ اس سے خرچ فقیر و غربا و درویشان
  خانقاہ و غیرہ وابستگان [OCR?] کی خوراک کے لیے نکلتا رہا۔ گویا کہ وہ دو من غلہ کئی سو من بن گیا" —
  two man of wheat in the household feeding the poor, the darweshes of the khanqah and its
  dependants [OCR?] daily for a year (pp. 254–255, folios 242–243).
- **Named khalifa and transmission.** Mulla Shah Qadiri is his khalifa (p. 242, folio 230; appendix
  row 15, p. 288). Pir Burhan, who came from Bukhara in Akbar's time, "لاہور کے بزرگوں میاں میر و
  شاہ بلاول سے قادریہ فیض پایا" — took Qadiri fayz from the Lahore elders Mian Mir and Shah Bilawal
  (p. 270, folio 258): independent confirmation of the row's Qadiri affiliation from outside the
  saint's own entry. Shaikh Abdullah Shah Baloch's chain "چار واسطوں کے ساتھ حضرت میاں میر بالا پیر
  لاہوریؒ کے ساتھ ملتا تھا", joined him through four intermediaries (p. 82, folio 70 [marker: 80]).
- **A five-step Qadiri chain running down to 1213 AH** (entry no. 152, pp. 221–222, folios 209–210):
  "سلسلہ قادریہ میں ان کا شجرہ … حضرت میاں میر بالا پیر کے ساتھ اس طرح پر ملتا ہے کہ سید بھاون شاہ
  مرید شاہ بلاق [OCR?] اور وہ مرید شاہ عبد الرشید لاہوری اور وہ مرید شیخ محسن شاہ اور وہ مرید شیخ
  محمد المشہور ملّا شاہ اور وہ مرید حضرت میاں میر لاہوری کے" — Bhawan Shah ← Shah Bilaq[OCR?] ← Shah
  Abd al-Rashid Lahori ← Shaikh Muhsin Shah ← Shaikh Muhammad, famous as Mulla Shah ← Mian Mir
  Lahori (p. 222). **Shaikh Muhsin Shah here shares a name with the "محسن شاہ" of the Chishti Sabiri
  chain the editor rejected for Ghore Shah** (p. 179, chunk 017) — a placement doubt, not a fact.
- **A hostile anecdote from a rival order's source.** Hafiz Tahir "حصول علم باطن کے لیے حضرت ملا شاہ
  قادری میاں میر بالاپیر کے خلیفہ کے پاس چند سال حاضر رہا" — attended Mulla Shah Qadiri, Mian Mir
  Bala Pir's khalifa, for some years — "مگر وہاں اس کو کچھ بہرہ نہ ملا اور سخت بے اعتقاد ہو کر اسلام
  سے بھی برگشتہ ہوگیا۔ رشتہ زنار گردن میں ڈال لیا اور فقرائے ہنود میں مل کر جا بجا گدائی کرنے لگا" —
  he got no benefit there, became faithless, apostatised, put on the zunnar and begged among Hindu
  faqirs, until Naushah restored him (p. 242, folio 230). The author's stated source is
  "کتاب تذکرہ نوشاہی" (p. 242) and he neither endorses nor softens it (chunk 023): to be cited as
  that tradition's view of the Mian Mir line, not as the book's judgement. Hafiz Tahir's own death:
  author "سال ایک ہزار ایک سو چھتیس" = 1136; editor 1097 AH[OCR?] (p. 243, folio 231).

**Legends & Miracles**
- "حضرت کے خوارق و کرامت کے حال میں شہزادہ دارا شکوہ نے ایک کتاب سکینۃ الاولیا نام لکھی ہے" — Prince
  Dara Shikoh wrote a whole book, *Sakinat al-Auliya*, on his karamat (p. 61, folio 49).
- **His own ruling against displaying karamat, inside a book of karamat** — the most citable line in
  this material. When news of his sister's grain miracle reached him: "آخر یہ خبر میاں میر بالا پیر
  کو ہوئی تو فرمایا کہ اظہار کرامت منع ہے اور خدا روزی دہندہ ہے۔ کیا ضرور ہے کہ اس طرح پر غلہ حاصل
  کیا جائے" — the display of karamat is forbidden, and God is the giver of sustenance; what need is
  there to obtain grain in this way? (p. 255, folio 243).

**Significance**
- Sources the book lists for him (p. 61, folio 49): *Safinat al-Auliya* 69–73; *Sakinat al-Auliya*
  (Tehran 1965); Abdul Hamid Lahori's *Badshah Nama* juz 1 at six separate pages; Kanbo's
  *Amal-e Salih* 3/357–360; Qani Thathawi's *Tuhfat al-Kiram*; Jahangir's *Tuzuk*.
- For the archive, the chain at p. 222 establishes that **the book treats Mian Mir as the root of a
  still-living Lahore Qadiri lineage**, not merely as a seventeenth-century grave; "میاں میر لاہوری"
  is a nisba of city, not of quarter, and nothing in that chunk touches his shrine, dates, urs or
  the Alam Ganj location.
- Co-indexed leads on his pages, not facts about him: "ملا شاہ بدخشی | ۵۶ ، ۵۷ ، ۵۸" (p. 330);
  "میاں منگو (والد شاہ سلیمان قادری لاہوری) | ۵۴" (p. 331); "محمد حیات (نبیرہ شاہ بلاول) | ۵۰"
  (p. 328); "شیخو پورہ | ۵۰" (p. 334). Folio 48 also carries
  "مجدد الف ثانی (احمد سرہندی) | ۴۷ ، ۴۸ ، ۱۱۶ …", "قادن قاضی | ۴۸", "عابد کبیر سید | ۴۸",
  "قلندر فاروقی قاضی | ۴۸ ، ۴۹", "کمال الدین محمد احسان | ۴۸ ، ۱۱۸ ، ۱۲۳ ، ۱۲۷ ، ۱۳۰" (pp. 322–327).

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

**B17. `shrine-of-mian-mir` — two location columns that do not describe one place, and a burial the
editor's own quotation forbids.** The editor's table gives 1875 "اندر احاطہ خانقاہ حضرت میاں میر"
against 1974 "کراچی ریلوے لائن اور میانی صاحب گاؤں کے درمیان" (p. 291, folio 279; chunk 028). The row
puts Mian Mir at **Alam Ganj (Dharampura)**, not Miani Sahib. **Do not let the 1974 column relocate
the archive's shrine**; the enclosure fact stands on the 1875 column alone. Compounding it: Bibi Jamal
Khatun, whose grave is what the enclosure fact is about, is placed by the author "شہر لاہور میں
**[illegible]** خانقاہ کے اندر" and dated 1000 AH, while the editor quotes Dara Shikoh for 27 Rabi
al-Awwal **1057 AH** and for "**در سیوستان موطن خود اقامت دارند و ہرگز از آں جا بجائے دیگر نرفتہ
اند**" — she never left Sehwan. **If the editor's quotation is right the Lahore burial cannot stand,
and the illegible khanqah name is then doubly unusable** (p. 255, folio 243, and حاشیہ 1; chunk 024).

- `shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal`, `shrine-of-mian-mir` — do not attach the unplaced Lahore graves of
  chunks 009 and 010 (Shah Kaku outside Delhi Gate, Jan Allah at Bagh Maharan Singh[OCR?], Abd
  al-Karim by Chauburji[OCR?], Abd al-Khaliq unlocated, Muhammad Arif 1071 AH, Muhammad Siddiq 1084 AH,
  Muhammad Salim 1151 AH) "on the strength of a shared city and a Chishti-Sabiri nisba". They belong
  on a candidate list.

**L16. `shrine-of-mian-mir` — the appendix's landmark is illegible.** "میاں میر نزد **[illegible]**
ریلوے سٹیشن" — "the name of the railway station cannot be given" (p. 287, folio 275; chunk 027).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "ملک پنجاب", "پنجاب"; refers to: the author's own time, "اس وقت", and the book's scope; author's text (1875), the preface "در اظہار باعث تالیف کتاب حدیقۃ الاولیاء"): the author says friends asked him to write "ایک اور مجموعہ مختصر اردو زبان میں ... جس میں ملک پنجاب کے اولیاء کا حال ہو۔ یعنی دہلی سے پشاور تک جس قدر علاقہ اس وقت پنجاب کے ساتھ متعلق ہے اور مشہور اولیاء کے مزار اس میں ہیں". The book defines its own unit: "پنجاب" as everything "from Delhi to Peshawar" that belonged to Punjab at the time of writing. This is a differently bounded unit in the book's own words, and both ends it names lie outside today's province (p. 37, folio 25).
- (book's word: "پنجاب کی سرزمین"; refers to: the author's selection rule for the Naqshbandi chaman, period not stated; author's text (1875)): after giving the Naqshbandi chain down to "خواجہ باقی باللہ دہلوی", the author says: "چونکہ بزرگان مرقوم الصدر سے کسی کا مزار پنجاب کی سرزمین میں نہ تھا۔ لہذا احوال مفصل ان کا قلم انداز ہوا اور خواجہ باقی باللہ سے آغاز کیا گیا". In other words, a tomb in Punjab is his test for inclusion (p. 127, folio 115).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the arrival of the Qadiri line, which the book places after his ancestors left Baghdad at the time of "ہلاکو خاں". No year is given in the text; author's text (1875)): "حضرت کے سلسلہ میں سے حضرت سید محمد غوث اوچی جیلی گیلانی اول پنجاب میں آئے". The same passage says he came "خراسان کے راستے ملتان میں" and settled "اوچ کے مقام پر". The editor's footnote (modern) on the arrival gives "بقول علی اصغر گیلانی آپ ۸۸۷ھ میں اوچ میں داخل ہوئے" (citing "خزینۃ الاصفیا ۱ / ۱۱۶ بحوالہ شجرۃ الانوار"). That date is the editor's, not the author's (p. 40, folio 28).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the lifetime of "شاہ معروف چشتی و قادری", who is entry 12; no date is stated in the hit; author's text (1875)): the Naushahi order spread in Punjab through him: "انہیں کی ذات بابرکات سے پنجاب میں سلسلہ نوشاہیہ نے فروغ پایا". The pronoun may refer to Shah Ma'ruf or to "سید مبارک حقانی", named just before. The text does not settle which (p. 50, folio 38).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the author's present for the "thousands" and the lifetime of Naushah for the founding; author's text (1875), entry 30 "شیخ حاجی محمد قادری المشہور بحضرت نوشہ گنج بخش"): "طریقہ نوشاہیہ جس کے فقیر پنجاب میں ہزاروں ہیں۔ ان سے شروع ہوا" (p. 70, folio 58).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the time of "خواجہ معین الدین حسن سجزی ثم الاجمیری", period not stated; author's text (1875), opening of the Chishti chaman): "یہ سلسلہ ہند اور پنجاب میں حضرت خواجہ معین الدین حسن سجزی سے شائع ہوا" (p. 85, folio 73).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the lifetime of Khwaja Sulaiman Taunsvi, "ولادت ۱۱۸۴ھ ... وفات ۱۲۶۷ھ" as the footnote gives them; editor's footnote (modern), on the khalifas of Nur Muhammad Maharvi): "پنجاب میں سلسلہ چشتیہ نظامیہ فخریہ کی زیادہ اشاعت حضرت خواجہ سلیمان تونسوی کے ذریعہ ہوئی" (p. 120, folio 108).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the time of "احمد شاہ ابدالی", as the footnote states; editor's footnote (modern), on Ghulam Murtaza Qasuri, citing "رسالہ مسائل فقہ قلمی مملوکہ حجی [OCR?] معین الدین لاہور"): "آپ نے پنجاب میں سکھ گردی سے تنگ آکر پشاور ہجرت فرمائی"; "احمد شاہ ابدالی جب پنجاب میں آیا تو اس نے یہاں کے علماء سے ایک مسئلہ کے بارے دریافت کیا". Ghulam Murtaza's name is among the ulama who answered (p. 152, folio 140).
- (book's word: "پنجاب", "خطہ"; refers to: the historiography of the region up to the editor's day, with no date given; editor's introduction (modern)): "اس اعتبار سے پنجاب سب سے محروم القسمت خطہ ہے۔ یہاں کی اب تک کوئی باقاعدہ اور تحقیقی تاریخ نہیں لکھی گئی"; those who wrote on "پنجاب کی تاریخ" gave only formal chapters and did not touch the Sufi tazkiras; the need is to fill the gaps in "پنجاب کی سیاسی، ثقافتی اور رُوحانی تاریخ" (p. 23, folio 11).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the editor's day, with no date given; editor's introduction (modern)): the editor has compiled a list of tazkiras, malfuzat and maktubat of "پنجاب کے صوفیہ کرام", titled "پنجاب کی تاریخ تصوف کے مآخذ". No comprehensive tazkira of "پنجاب کے صوفیہ" has come to his notice. The Mufti's general tazkiras gave Punjab representation, and without them "آج ہمیں پنجاب میں کوئی عالم و عارف نظر ہی نہ آتا" (p. 24, folio 12).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: "سلطان محمود غزنوی سے لے کر ۱۲۹۲ھ / ۱۸۷۵ء تک", the span as the editor states it; editor's introduction (modern)): the book holds "پنجاب کے ۲۴۴ [OCR?] صوفیہ کرام کے حالات" in seven chamans (p. 31, folio 19). It also takes in some well-known Sufis "جن کا پنجاب سے کوئی تعلق نہیں تھا" (p. 32, folio 20).
- (book's word: "پنجاب", "اکابر پنجاب"; refers to: the Sikh period as the author witnessed it, with no dates given; editor's introduction (modern), assessing the book): among the book's merits, the editor says the Mufti recorded "پنجاب کے اعیان کی سکھوں کے ہاتھوں بے عزتی اور مشائخ کے مقابر کی تباہی" as an eyewitness, and that his own family had been a target of "سکھ گردی". By preserving his contemporaries he did "پنجاب پر بڑا احسان" (p. 33, folio 21). Some Punjab shaikhs are found only in this book (p. 32, folio 20). Among the book's weaknesses, the editor says the Mufti wrote up whatever "اکابر پنجاب" came to mind "ایک نشست میں" (p. 33, folio 21).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the book's scope; title page and table of contents of the modern edition): the subtitle reads "پنجاب کے اکابر صُوفیہ کا مُستند تذکرہ" (p. 1 and p. 9, folio not stated). The table of contents heads a closing section "حالات بزرگان پنجاب / جن کی وفات کا سن و تاریخ بپایہ ثبوت نہیں پہنچا" (entries ۲۰۵ onward) (p. 20, folio 8).


- (book's word: "پنجاب کا صوبہ", "پنجاب"; refers to: the Ghaznavid period — "سلطان محمود کے مرنے کے بعد جب مسعود تخت نشین ہوا", then Maudud — as the book states; author's text (1875)): Masud's young son Majdud "پنجاب کا صوبہ قرار پایا", with Ayaz appointed as his tutor (اتالیق). Here `صوبہ` names the office Majdud was given, not a bounded unit. After Masud died and Maudud took the throne, Majdud rebelled and set up "اپنی سلطنت علیحدہ ہندوستان میں". Maudud marched on him, Majdud died suddenly on the day of Eid during the siege of Lahore, "اور مودود نے اپنا تسلط پنجاب میں کر لیا" (p. 193, folio 181).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: "سلطان مسعود غزنوی کے وقت", death given as "سال چار سو اڑتالیس ۴۴۸"; author's text (1875), in a passage that opens "صاحب کتاب تحفۃ الواصلین لکھتا ہے کہ" — where the citation ends is not marked): Shaikh Muhammad Ismail Muhaddith Lahori, a Bukhari sayyid, settled in Lahore and taught hadith and tafsir. He was the first of the callers to Islam to come to Lahore, thousands converted, "اور دین اسلام ان کے طفیل سے پنجاب میں شائع ہوا" (p. 191, folio 179).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: modern historiography, period not stated; editor's footnote (modern), on the entry above): "صاحب نزہۃ الخواطر جن کا پنجاب کے سلسلہ میں سب سے بڑا ماخذ خزینۃ الاصفیا ہے اور انہوں نے عموماً خزینۃ الاصفیا کی روایات بغیر کسی تحقیق کے شامل کتاب بھی کر لی ہیں". In the same footnote the editor calls the author's source "تحفۃ الواصلین ایک وضعی تذکرہ" and concludes "شیخ اسمٰعیل محدث لاہوری کا صحیح سال وفات ۹۸۰ ھ ہے نہ کہ ۴۴۸ ھ" (pp. 191–192, folios 179–180).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the Ghaznavid sultanate in Punjab up to "شاہ بہرام کے وقت"; quoted source: تحفۃ الواصلین, as retold in the author's text (1875) under the Shahid Ganj entry): "جب سلطنت سلاطین غزنویہ کی پنجاب میں ہوگئی اور لاہور دار الحکومت قرار پایا تو مدت تک سلاطین غزنویہ کی سلطنت بخوبی پنجاب میں رہی۔ آخر جب شاہ بہرام کے وقت آپس میں سلاطین غزنویہ کے فساد ہوا تو پنجاب کی حکومت بالکل ضعیف ہوگئی". Raja Anangpal then took Lahore, and "چند سے [OCR?]" there was Hindu rule until an army from Ghazni returned (p. 264, folio 252). The editor's separate verdict on this source is in the previous bullet (p. 192, folio 180).
- (book's word: "پنجاب", "ملک پنجاب"; refers to: Chingiz Khan's pursuit of "شہزادہ جلال الدین خوارزمی", date not stated; quoted source: "کتاب تذکرہ حمیدیہ", as reported in the author's text (1875) under the Bibiyan Pakdaman entry): "چنگیز خان کی فوج اس کے تعاقب میں پنجاب میں داخل ہوئی۔ تمام ملک پنجاب انہوں نے غارت کر لیا۔ شہر لاہور کے لوگ دو مہینے تک ان کے ساتھ لڑتے رہے". The author adds that this account "مشہور نہیں ہے۔ شاید کوئی اس پر یقین نہ کرے گا۔ مگر عجب نہیں ہے کہ ایسا ہوا ہو" (p. 248, folio 236). The editor's addenda give a separate reading for Lahore only: "شہاب الدین نسوی ... نے چنگیز خان کے لاہور پر حملے کا ذکر ہی نہیں کیا" (p. 352, folio 340).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the Sikh disorders, "قوم سکھ", date not stated; Karam Shah's death is given both as "سال ایک ہزار دو سو" in words and as "۱۲۰۱ ھ" in the chronogram line; author's text (1875)): Shaikh Karam Shah Qureshi had lived in Lahore. "جب غارت گر ملا [OCR?] قوم سکھ نے پنجاب میں ہنگامہ غارت گری گرم کیا تو یہ بزرگ لکھنؤ کو چلا گیا". He was killed by bandits near Shahjahanpur on the way back (p. 190, folio 178).
- (book's word: "پنجاب" in "بعد غارت پنجاب"; refers to: "جب احمد بادشاہ درانی پہلی مرتبہ لاہور کی تسخیر کو آیا"; author's text (1875), introduced "نقل ہے"): "جب بعد غارت پنجاب بادشاہ کابل کو چلا گیا". A Lahore man then told the saint (Haji Muhammad Said Lahori) that "افغانوں" had looted his house and carried off his daughter to Kabul (p. 214, folio 202). The editor's footnote on the same page dates the first attack on Lahore only: "احمد شاہ ابدالی کا لاہور پر پہلا حملہ (۱۱۶۱ھ) ۱۷۴۸ء کو ہوا".
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: "۱۱۶۱ھ/۱۷۴۸ء" to "۱۱۸۷ھ/۱۷۷۳ء", as the footnote states; editor's footnote (modern)): rejecting the date the author gives for Ahmad Shah Durrani's farman to Mufti Muhammad Mukarram ("۱۱۲۰ھ [OCR?]"), the editor writes "یہ سنہ غلط ہے۔ احمد شاہ ابدالی نے ۱۱۶۱ھ/۱۷۴۸ء میں پنجاب پر مختلف حملے شروع کیے اور ۱۱۸۷ھ/۱۷۷۳ء میں فوت ہو گیا" (p. 277, folio 265).
- (book's word: "مہاراجہ رنجیت سنگھ والی پنجاب"; refers to: Ranjit Singh's rule, date not stated; Mastan Shah's death given as "سال ایک ہزار دو سو ستتر ہجری"; author's text (1875)): "مہاراجہ رنجیت سنگھ والی پنجاب کو کمال اعتقاد اس کی نسبت تھا". He offered thousands of rupees, which the majzub Mastan Shah refused (p. 246, folio 234).
- (book's word: "پنجاب کے مالک فرنگی"; refers to: the day "جس روز راجہ رنجیت سنگھ مرا تھا", with "نو برس" more of "یہ سلطنت" — the book's "سکھوں کی سلطنت"; author's text (1875), reporting the majzub Taj Shah's saying): "سکھوں کی سلطنت کی خرابی کا حال پہلے ہی اس نے بیان کر دیا تھا ... نو برس اور یہ سلطنت رہے گی پھر پنجاب کے مالک فرنگی ہو جائیں گے". The unit here is the book's "سکھوں کی سلطنت", passing to "فرنگی" (p. 244, folio 232).
- (book's word: "پنجاب", "ملک پنجاب"; refers to: the book's own scope, written in the author's present; the closing chronograms read "۱۲۹۲ھ"; author's text (1875)): chaman 5 will hold "ان بزرگوں کا ذکر ... جو پنجاب میں عالم و فاضل صاحب نام گذرے ہیں" (p. 191, folio 179). Chaman 7 is headed "عَورات صالحات کے ذکر میں جو پنجاب میں گذر چکی ہیں" (p. 247, folio 235). A later section is headed "حالات بزرگان پنجاب جن کی وفات کا سن و تاریخ بپایہ ثبوت نہیں پہنچا" (p. 261, folio 249). In the closing, the author says his Persian *خزینۃ الاصفیا* was too large, so "شائقین ملک پنجاب کا یہ مطلب حاصل نہیں ہوتا تھا کہ کتنے بزرگ پنجاب کے ملک میں صاحب طریقت گذرے ہیں", a difficulty this short Urdu book removes (p. 280, folio 268).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the book's scope, chronogram "۱۲۹۲ھ"; quoted source: chronogram "از مفتی غلام صفدر صاحب لاہوری خلف مؤلف"): "جو خدا کے دوست تھے پنجاب میں   حال ان کا اس سے ہوتا ہے عیان" (p. 282, folio 270).
- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the scholars of Punjab "شیخ حامد سے لے کر حضرت شیخ بہاؤالدین زکریا ملتانی تک"; editor's footnote (modern)): Maulana Muhammad Aqil Lahori's *تحفۃ المسلمین* records Shaikh Hamid Qari's sayings and also gives "پنجاب کے اکابر علما کے حالات" across that span. The editor says its rare manuscript is in his own library (p. 278, folio 266).
