Muhammad Baqir. Lahore, 1965` (p. 235, folio 225) and `Ed. Lahore, 1312` (p. 238, folio 228); Lahore is also recorded as the destination of the Jama'at-i-Islami in 1939 (pp. 214–231, folios 203–220).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated — "the early Muslim saints in general, and the Chishti masha'ikh in particular"; author's argument): "Not only for Gujarat, Bengal, but also for Uttar Pradesh, Punjab, and the Deccan does the significance of the early Muslim saints in general, and the Chishti masha'ikh in particular, mark a particular kind of South Asian spiritual authority that is not only political and religious but also cultural"; the authors describe "a spectrum of spiritually charged spaces or sites" that are "devotional loci for Indian Muslims from several classes, and also for Hindus" (p. 110, folio 97). Punjab is one of five regions named together; nothing specific to Punjab is said.
- (book's word: "the Punjab", which the book calls one of "two provinces"; refers to: Akbar's reign, the Fatehpur Sikri phase, which he "abandoned ... by 1585" — as the book states; author's argument): through Shaykh Salim and "Shaykh Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar in the Punjab", "Through a twofold, redoubled Chishti loyalty, Akbar could anchor his imperial legitimacy in two provinces adjacent to Uttar Pradesh, the Punjab and Rajasthan, both of which also happened to be crucial to the political-military ambitions of his reign" (pp. 112–113, folios 99–100). The book's own unit is a Mughal-period "province"; its extent is not stated.
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the eighteenth century"; book's historical prose): "The Nizami Chishtis came to prominence in the eighteenth century, principally in Delhi, the Deccan, and Punjab, through masters who were related to but in tension with local political leaders" (p. 119, folio 106).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "Punjabi Hindus"; refers to: Shaykh Sulayman Taunsawi's time, "as British colonialism began to take hold of India" — Taunsawi is "(d. 1850)" on the same page; book's historical prose): Taunsawi "urged his followers to participate in resistance against Sikh aggression in the Punjab, and so, apparently for the first time in the annals of Chishti history, we have spiritual biographies drawn into partisanship on behalf of one political group (Muslim) over against another (non-Muslim)"; "Notable, however, is the effort of Shah Sulayman to maintain cordial relations with Punjabi Hindus even while he opposed their Sikh neighbors" (p. 121, folio 108). The supporting endnote (n. 10) cites M. Zameeruddin Siddiqi, "The Resurgence of the Chishti Silsilah in the Punjab during the Eighteenth Century" (p. 214, folio 203).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "the southern Punjab"; refers to: Taunsawi "(d. 1850)", his successor Muhammad 'Aqil "(d. 1814)" and the latter's disciple Gul Muhammad Ahmadpuri "(d. 1827)" — as printed; book's historical prose): Taunsawi "consolidated the order by stressing learning through establishment of institutions: not only the academy called Dar al- 'Ulum at Taunsa on the upper Indus, but also other religious schools throughout the Punjab"; 'Aqil and Ahmadpuri "both of whom founded religious schools in the southern Punjab that provided free instruction and lodging to hundreds of students" (pp. 121–122, folios 108–109).
- (book's word: "the upper Punjab"; refers to: "the nineteenth century"; book's historical prose): the line from Shah Mirra "resurfaces in the nineteenth century in the figure of Hafiz Sayyid Muhammad 'Ali Khayrabadi, marking the expansion of the Nizami branch of Chishti Sufism through disciples of Shaykh Sulayman Taunsawi in the upper Punjab" (p. 71, folio 57).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "the colonial period"; author's argument): "If we were to follow only the dictates of political history and the criteria of social science, the test case for the Chishti Nizami Sulaymaniyya lineage in the colonial period might seem to be the battles over shrines in the Punjab"; the example given is Taunsa, and "Political power struggles internal to the Sulaymaniyya can be traced to British manipulation and also the rivalry of local Sikhs, Hindus, and Muslim reformers" (p. 124, folio printed "Ill" [text-layer?] [111]). The authors then set this political reading aside: "political contestation is only part of the narrative".
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: present day ("continues to be intoned"); ethnographic present): "The name Qalandar continues to be intoned in popular qawwali lyrics of the Punjab, and it has been appropriated by a mainstream Sufi group, the Indian Qalandari order centered at Kakori near Lucknow" (p. 36, folio 22). Which side of the border is not stated.

## From Saiyid Athar Abbas Rizvi, A History of Sufism in India, vol. 1

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/rizvi_history_of_sufism_india_1.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Place-level material: LAHORE

*The book's place-level material on "LAHORE" (35 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi, bibi-pak-daman, darbar-abul-muali-qadri, data-darbar, ganj-e-inayat-sarkar, ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed, gurdwara-babay-nanki, gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore, gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan, gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang, gurdwara-chowmala-sahib, gurdwara-dera-sahib, gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji, gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore, gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh, gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian, jain-mandir-lahore, khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz, krishna-mandir-ravi-road, loh-temple-lava-temple, madho-lal-hussain, mazar-e-iqbal, peer-makki, samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh, shah-jamal, shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh, shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal, shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari, shrine-of-mian-mir, shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib, shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri, tahir-bandagi-qadri, tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak, valmik-mandir-naqi-road, wasif-ali-wasif** — **LAHORE, the archive's 35 rows.** What the book actually attaches to the bare name: an eleventh-century city hostile to its own saint — "a number of mystics and scholars who had settled in Lahore were strongly hostile to the views of its author", "Some Lahore sufis believed that gnosis emanated from inspiration", "a ‘captive among uncongenial folk’ in Lahore" (p. 131, folio 113); a route terminus, "one of the main routes from Multan to Lahore and Delhi" (p. 158, folio 140) and "Qazi Shu'aib, went to Lahore" (p. 157, folio 139); a capital, "Lahore had become the capital", with "the Khwaja reached Delhi from Lahore" and "His father, Ahmad, migrated from Lahore to Delhi" (pp. 135, 140, 145; folios 117, 122, 127); a city destroyed and a merchant colony — "the destruction of Lahore, by the Mongols in 1241" and "A group of Lahore merchants visited Gujarat" (pp. 241–242, folios 221–222); a city Babur conquered in 1523-24 (p. 367, folio 345); a compass bearing, "about forty miles south-west of Lahore" (p. 407, folio 383); and one named building that is not a shrine, "the Padishahi mosque at Lahore" (p. 339, folio 317, footnote 3), inside Goldziher on relic worship. **Chunk 001's Doubts records four further Lahore mentions in pp. 1–29 whose individual page references are not carried in the Pass 1 text; the conversion is recorded at chunk level (pp. 1–29) and a human must recover the pages.** **NOT converted: the two Lahore-as-imprint mentions — "published in Lahore in 1909" and "Oriental College Magazine, Lahore X/1" (pp. 300, 310; folios 278, 288), and the Lahore imprints throughout the bibliography (pp. 442, 446, 452, 454; folios 418, 422, 428, 430) — which are publication data, not places in the narrative.**

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: Ghaznawid rule, "Yamain al-Dawla Mahmud (388/998-421/1030)" and "Bahram Shah (1118-52)" as printed; book's historical prose): Mahmud "established Ghaznawid rule over Khurasan, Afghanistan and the Panjab"; after two defeats by 'Ala'u'd-Din of Ghur, "Bahram Shah was forced to flee to the Panjab" (p. 83, folio 67).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: Mahmud of Ghazna's annexation and after, with Hujwiri reaching Lahore "in c. 1035, while Sultan Mas'ud I (1031-41) reigned in Ghazna"; book's historical prose): "the annexation of the Panjab by Mahmud of Ghazna and its incorporation into his empire prompted many sufis to settle in the area"; Khattali "ordered his disciple, Shaikh Husain Zinjani to move to the Panjab", and later sent Hujwiri after him (p. 130, folio 112).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "the middle of the twelfth century", after "'Ala'u'd-Din Husain of Ghur in 1150-51, followed by the Ghuzz invasion six years later"; book's historical prose): Baba Farid's ancestors "came from Kabul, but migrated to the Panjab in the middle of the twelfth century" (p. 156, folio 138); the destruction of Ghazna and the Ghuzz invasion "drove a large number of people from that region to the Panjab for safety"; the book names "The Ghaznawid Sultan of the Panjab" as the one who made Farid's father Qazi of Kahtwal (p. 157, folio 139).
- (book's word: "the southern part of the Panjab"; refers to: "1175", as printed; book's historical prose): "A hundred and fifty years later in 1175 Mu'izzu'd-Din Muhammad bin Sam seized Multan, establishing firm control and Sunni rule over the southern part of the Panjab" (p. 128, folio 110).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: after Jalalu'd-Din's defeat "in November 1221" and Chingiz Khan's march back west; book's historical prose): Chingiz Khan's "absence enabled Itutmish [OCR?] [Iltutmish] to consolidate power in the Panjab, seven years later routing from Sind his rival, Nasiru'd-Din Qubacha" (p. 152, folio 134).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "previous years" before Muhammad bin Tughluq's scheme "attempted in 1327"; book's historical prose): the Sultan raised "the slogan of jihad against the Mongols, who in previous years had devastated the Panjab and the region around Delhi" (p. 193, folio 175).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "Timur's invasion in 1398-99", as printed; book's historical prose): "Timur's invasion in 1398-99 devastated both the Panjab and Delhi" (p. 290, folio 270).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: the time of Saiyid Jalalu'd-Din Bukhari, Makhdum-i Jahaniyan, born "15 Sha'ban 707/9 February 1308", and of "Sultan Firuz Shah Tughlug" [OCR?] [Tughluq]; book's historical prose): the Suhrawardiyya order "spread from Uch to Gujarat, the Panjab and Kashmir even extending its influence to the Delhi of Sultan Firuz Shah Tughlug [OCR?] [Tughluq]" (p. 299, folio 277).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: the time of "Mongol domination of Central Asia and Iran", Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya and Baba Farid; book's historical prose): Qalandariyyas and Jawaliqis passed through Multan, where they "often came into conflict with Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya"; "From the Punjab, the displeased qalandars moved to Delhi creating scenes wherever they saw a khanqah. Some went as far east as Bengal" (p. 325, folio 303).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "the fifteenth century", with "The Mongol invasions between the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries" behind it; book's historical prose): "In the fifteenth century, the Panjab enjoyed a peaceful period due to Afghan rule. The Mongol invasions between the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries had devastated portions of the Panjab and the Afghans had successfully contributed to its re-settlement and urbanization by founding many new towns and promoting trade and commerce" (p. 408, folio 384).
- (book's word: "the Panjab", "west Panjab"; refers to: "About 1520" and Babur's third invasion "in 926/1520", as printed; book's historical prose): Guru Nanak returned from eastern India "only to find the Panjab plunged into a number of significant political crises"; Babur "started unsuccessful negotiations with Ibrahim to annex west Panjab to Kabul. After two minor raids around the region between the Indus and Jhelum, Babur invaded a third time over the Panjab in 926/1520 reaching as far as Sialkot"; a footnote says Daulat Khan "Gradually he also became the governor of the Panjab and in [1523 [OCR?] invited Babur to invade India" (p. 409, folio 385).
- (book's word: "Panjabi", "the Panjab"; refers to: "Guru Nanak's period"; quoted source, W.H. McLeod, *Guru Nanak and the Sikh religion*, quoted so that Rizvi can reject it): "the Panjabi sufism of Guru Nanak's period had evidently departed radically from the classical pattern of Arab and Persian Sufism"; most Muslims Nanak met "would, however, represent in varying degrees the blend of modified orthodoxy and debased Sufism which was dominant … in the Muslim community of the Panjab during this period" (pp. 419–420, folios 395–396).
- (book's word: "Panjabis", "the Panjab"; refers to: "the time of Guru Nanak"; author's argument, against McLeod): "all literate Panjabis, both Hindus and Muslims, at the time of Guru Nanak were conversant with" 'Attar, Rumi and Sa'di; McLeod "seems to be unaware of Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Quddus Gangohi who lived in Shahabad, his disciple, Shaikh Jalal of Thaneswar, and the Qadiris who had made the Panjab the main centre of their activities" (p. 420, folio 396).

## From Omar Kasmani, Queer Companions

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/kasmani_queer_companions.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Place-level material: Lahore

*The book's place-level material on "Lahore" (35 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

  - **A terror attack on a Lahore shrine changed security at Sehwan.** "A great deal of what I had
    observed in 2009 was already changing as early as 2011. A terror attack at Lahore's most
    prominent shrine had led to security measures being beefed up at major Sufi sites across the
    country." (p. 104, folio 87) **The shrine is not named and the attack is not dated: (not
    stated)**; the 2011 date attaches to the tightening at Sehwan, not to the attack.
  - **Lahore as the source of Sehwan's pilgrim traffic.** "special trains like the Jhule Lal Express
    were initiated to facilitate the annual journey of pilgrims connecting Sehwan with Lahore, the
    capital of Punjab" (p. 70, folio 53); the Bukhari brothers "would go on to organize pilgrimages
    from Lahore to Sehwan, reserving entire trains for their annual journeys. Private patronage such
    as theirs greatly complemented the state's project in Sehwan as much as it was motivated by it."
    (p. 61, folio 44)
  - **Lahore as a place fakirs go to and come from.** Baba boarded a bus and arrived "at Data Darbar,
    Lahore's most prominent shrine complex", finding mentorship under a fakir and staying about a
    year (p. 65, folio 48); "It was the time of the saint's festival, and countless other fakirs had
    gathered in the city." (p. 65, folio 48) A "visiting fakir from Lahore who in the autumn of 2011
    was in Sehwan to perform his duty at the shrine of Lal" kept "his fakir retreat by the banks of
    the river" (p. 195, folio 178, ch. 5 n. 9). Baba's fakir story "traverses Sufi shrines across the
    Punjab" (p. 73, folio 56).
  - **Lahore after expulsion from a Sehwan lodge.** Murad "relocated to Lahore" and was reported at
    "Bibiyan-pak-daman, a shrine in Lahore dedicated to women saints and particularly revered by the
    Shiʿa" (p. 125, folio 108); later, "imparting the same knowledge of tariqat… to others in Lahore.
    There too his status is the same" (p. 136, folio 119); "Living on the outskirts of Lahore, he had
    visited the shrine a few times only to pay his respects" (p. 141, folio 124). The young woman he
    married was "a young pilgrim visiting from Lahore" whom he "had consulted at the shrine of Bodlo"
    (p. 125, folio 108).
  - **Lahore shrine economics, cited not described.** "For an account of the distribution of shrine
    revenues and the economic dimension of a Sufi shrine in Lahore, see Strothmann (2016, chapters 5
    and 6)." (p. 189, folio 172, ch. 1 n. 26) **The book does not say which shrine.**
  - **Lahore as a place of publication.** `Chishti, Muhammad A. 2008. Syed Usman Marwandi
    al-­Maruf Hazrat Lal Shahbaz Qalandar: Hayat, Afkar-­o-­talimat […]. Lahore: Haq.` (p. 205,
    folio 188); `Schimmel, Annemarie. 2003. Mystical Dimensions of Islam. Lahore: Sang-­e-­Meel.`
    (p. 215, folio 198).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "central Punjab"; refers to: "the colonial period" set against "the postcolonial state", as the book reports them; quoted source, Umber bin Ibad 2019): "Though focusing only on central Punjab, Ibad notes that in the colonial period, “the state did not try to control shrines directly through its bureaucracy”; however, the postcolonial state has gone further, especially by virtue of implementing a unique politico-religious ideology" — in his view, a desire "to harness the pluralistic traditions of shrines in favor of “representing a Singular Islam”" (Ibad 2019, introduction) (p. 69, folio 52).
- (book's word: "central Punjab"; refers to: stated in the present tense as a general condition, framed by Baba's childhood in a Punjab village "Decades on" from the 2012–2013 tellings; period otherwise not stated; author's argument): "Being a child in a poor rural family in central Punjab, one who is regarded as intersex means being given to particular bodily horizons, oriented to certain futures more than others"; "a most likely trajectory for a child such as Baba would have eventually involved a life with the khwaja-sara"; such children, "Given male names at birth and in most cases also registered as sons", "do not extend the social promise of family lines that cisgender sons are expected to offer" (p. 73, folio 56).
- (book's word: "parts of Sindh and Punjab"; "Rural Punjab and Sindh" in the cited title; refers to: period not stated — the study is dated 2010 in the References; quoted source, Lubna Chaudhry): "In a study of women and poverty in parts of Sindh and Punjab, Lubna Chaudhry notes how unmarried girls in their teens and early twenties are most vulnerable. She points to discriminatory food portions within the family and limited access to health care, in addition to a lack of share in economic resources, even those that involve their labor" (p. 191, folio 174, ch. 2 n. 24; the study is "Women and Poverty: Salient Findings from a Gendered Analysis of a Quasi-Anthropological Study in Rural Punjab and Sindh", 2010, p. 205, folio 188).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the eighteenth century"; book's historical prose, hedged "It is possible"): on the Nanakpanth among Sindh's Hindus: "The Nanakpanth would have been initiated later. It is possible that the Aroras imported this cult from Punjab during their migrations in Sindh in the eighteenth century. The Nanakpanth is a form of Sikhism that does not recognize the authority of the Khalsa" (p. 43, folio 30).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the second half of the nineteenth century"; book's historical prose, citing Oberoi 1994): "the first attempt to rationalize and uniformize the Sikh tradition was implemented. This process of normalizing the Sikh tradition was centred on Punjab, and it did not concern the Sindhi Nanakpanthis who were heterodox Sikhs" (p. 53, folio 40).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated, historical; author's argument citing Lorenzen 1996): "The Nirguni tradition was more influential in Northern India, especially in Punjab, where the Sikh tradition historically derived from the Nirguni tradition of the Bhakti" (p. 89, folio 76).
- (book's word: "the southern present Punjab"; refers to: an unstated past time ("at the time when"); author's argument): the shared devotional culture "would be called Sindhian culture, which refers to a set of social, religious and cultural traits that existed in the Sindhi area at the time when it encompassed the southern present Punjab, and also the territories of Rajasthan and Gujarat" (p. 53, folio 40). The book does not say whether "present Punjab" means the Pakistani province. The Rajasthan/Gujarat half is under `not attached`.
- (book's word: "Southern Punjab", "South Punjab"; refers to: present day for the language; "the Middle Ages" for the historical claim; author's argument, in an endnote, the glossary and the main text): Ch. 6 n. 11: "The Siraiki language is mainly spoken in Southern Punjab, with Multan as capital. Interestingly, this area was a part of Sindh during the Middle Ages" (p. 216, folio 203). Glossary: Siraiki is "a language spoken in Northern Sindh and Southern Punjab" (p. 220, folio 207). In the main text, for some specialists the Siraiki of Northern Sindh is "a distinct language from the Siraiki of South Punjab" (p. 171, folio 158).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument, in an endnote and Annex 2): "the Chishtiyya was not widespread in Sindh, while it is dominant in Punjab and North India" (General conclusion n. 1, p. 217, folio 204). "The Chishtiyya is usually considered to be one of the most powerful Sufi orders in Northern India and Punjab" (p. 227, folio 214). The book does not say which Punjab.
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the Pax Britannica imposed by the colonial rule", and present-day scholarship; author's argument): scholars of Sindhi studies treat Sindh's religious encounters "as if comparable dynamics were not implemented in other regions, be they neighbouring Sindh, such as Punjab, Rajasthan or Gujarat, or elsewhere". "During the Pax Britannica imposed by the colonial rule, the exchange of goods and products was reinforced between the different regions of the north-western Indian subcontinent, including Punjab, Rajasthan, Gujarat and Sindh". Beyond the merchants there were "networks of circulating knowledge, and especially religious knowledge" (p. 196, folio not stated). The book does not give this colonial "Punjab" any boundary.

    (p. 28, folio not stated); as the home of a Nath-sect informant (p. 138, folio not stated); and as
    the index headword `Lahore, 129` (p. 351, folio not stated). **The book attaches no shrine, tomb,
    saint, ʿurs or custodian to Lahore anywhere.** The attachment is place-level and carries nothing
    about any of the thirty-five sites.
  - **`darbar-malik-ahmad-ayaz` is deliberately NOT on this head.** Its `location_short` does not
    contain the string "Lahore" — the survey text records "Shah Alam Market" and says "No city,
    district, tehsil or province is stated anywhere in the survey for the shrine itself." A join on
    `location_short` therefore does not reach it, and the ruled count of 35 is reached without it.
    Recorded so the omission is visible and a human can rule. See Doubts.

- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "Currently", the time of writing; endnote): "the Punjab has 8 reserved minority seats out of 371" (the same note gives Sindh 9 of 168; the note cites the provincial assembly websites) (p. 28, folio not stated, endnote 5 to p. 15).
- (book's word: "Punjab Textbooks Board"; refers to: a class V Urdu textbook dated "Lahore 2002"; author's argument citing Nayyar and Salim 2005): in Pakistani schoolbooks "the Hindu" is not only a danger to national security but "has always been an enemy of Islam" (p. 20, folio not stated). The endnote gives the source as "“Hindū hameśah se islām kā duśman rahā hai,” Urdu class V, Punjab Textbooks Board, Lahore 2002 (see Nayyar and Salim 2005, 20)" (p. 28, folio not stated, endnote 7).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "the eighteenth century" for the Bhatia and Khatri migration, period not stated for Thakur's general remark; quoted source, Thakur 1997, and Cook 2010 as reported): migrants "from Rajputanta[text-layer?] [Rajputana?] and the Punjab" who "professed Lohana occupations have acquired membership of the Lohana caste" (Thakur 1997, 68f.); "The Bhatia and the Khatris, for example, arrived in the eighteenth century from cities in the Punjab—like Multan—and were ultimately absorbed as Lohanas (Cook 2010, 135)" (pp. 80–81, folio not stated).

  `data-darbar` above and are not repeated here.

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Punjabi villages"; refers to: period not stated, ethnographic present of Werth's chapter; editors' introduction, Werbner and Basu, summarising Lukas Werth's chapter 4): "This is reflected even at the most local level, in the countless shrines to unknown saints found on the outskirts of Punjabi villages. Against the unique biographies of named saints, these anonymous shrines, Lukas Werth discloses here, call for explanation"; their anonymity is "a key feature, he suggests, of their placement as mediators between the dangerous forces of nature and cultural continuity" (p. 23, folio 12). Werth's own fieldwork is at Rajpur in the salt range (see Sweep notes); the generalisation to "Punjabi villages" is the editors'.
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; contributor's endnote, Lukas Werth, ch. 4, n. 9): "I could not find in this case expressive allusions to Khidr, who is often called Zinda pir in Punjab, because he also is thought to have disappeared alive (see Schimmel 1975: 202)" (p. 101, folio 90).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; contributor's argument, Jamal Malik, ch. 9): "The cult of the holy man (pîr) and the associated folk piety is particularly evident in the countryside, where major landlords and large-scale land concentrations are crucial features, especially in Punjab and Sindh. In many cases, the local pîr represents the local landlord or is at least closely related to him" (p. 199, folio 188).

## From Shemeem Burney Abbas, The Female Voice in Sufi Ritual

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/abbas_female_voice_sufi_ritual.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

- **Saint**
  - **A death date IS printed in the book and is destroyed in the text layer.** "I noticed the same
    feature at the Hazrat Mian Mir (d. [text-layer?: numeral dropped — the book prints a death date
    here that does not extract]) shrine in Lahore, which I visited this summer" (p. 21, folio not
    stated). **This is the single most important number a human must recover for this row.** 1635 has
    not been supplied.
  - **The book gives him a silsila and a city.** "Akbar's great-grandson Dara Shikoh, a devotee of
    Hazrat Mian Mir, a **Qadiriya** Sufi mystic of **Lahore**, also patronized Hindu mystics
    (saniyasis)" (p. 197, folio not stated). **This agrees with the archive row's "Qadiri"; it is the
    book's own statement and is recorded as such.**
  - The index files him under `Suﬁ Saints belonging to Pakistan-Indian silsilās` as `Mir, Hazrat Mian,
    xix, xx` — **both printed locators survive and both are front matter** (p. 239, folio not stated).
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - A fieldwork site in the Lahore group (p. 20, folio not stated).
  - **Collapse, restoration and conversion to mosque use.** "The shrine had fallen to pieces almost a
    decade ago. It has been recently restored and is also used as a mosque" (p. 21, folio not stated).
    **"The same feature" refers back to the Data Darbar passage — extension and renovation into a
    mosque, with Friday congregational prayer (pp. 20–21, folios not stated).** "Almost a decade ago"
    survives because it is spelled out; **"this summer" is undated and the restoration year is (not
    stated).** No custodian, no Auqaf, no ʿurs: all **(not stated)**.
- **The women of his household — the reason this row matters to this book.**
  - "Rizvi mentions the saintly mothers of Shaikh Abdul Haqq Muhaddis Dehlavi, Khawaja Baqi Billah, and
    Mian Mir, who played a role in making their sons great Sufis. He also mentions Bibi Jamal Khatun
    ([numerals dropped, text-layer]), sister of Mian Mir, who, according to Dara Shikoh, was the Rabia
    of her times" (p. 192, folio not stated). **Bibi Jamal Khatun's dates are printed in the book and
    destroyed here: (not stated). Neither his mother nor his sister is otherwise named or described.**

### Place-level material: Lahore

*The book's place-level material on "Lahore" (35 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** (the book's "Lahore" — 35 rows)
  - **Lahore is the book's densest shrine city and the place-level material is substantial.** "In the
    major mosques in Pakistan, for instance, there is a small space where women can go and pray on
    Friday or on religious festivals such as the Eid. In the local mohallā mosques there is no
    possibility for a female to offer her ritual prayers. The domain is exclusively for male
    participation. Thus, the important spheres of religious and spiritual participation for women are
    the Sufi shrines" (p. 18, folio not stated) — the sentence that makes every shrine in this list a
    women's space in the book's argument.
  - **Hazrī at the city's shrines, before and after partition.** Lahori "also mentions women musicians
    who performed hazrī at the Sufi shrines in Lahore, especially at the time of the urs", and uses
    "the very term ''hazrī'' in connection with qawwāls who sang at the Sufi shrines in Lahore before
    the partition of [text-layer?: year dropped] and after it" (p. 90, folio not stated). A cited work
    carries the practice in its title: "Lahori, Tahir. ''Aulīyā ke Urs.'' In Sohna Shehr Lahore.
    Lahore: Sang-e Meel Publications, [year dropped]" (p. 227, folio not stated).
  - **The city's saints as a genre of local literature.** "Malik, M. Latif. Aulīyā-e Lahore. Lahore:
    Sang-e Meel Publications (Institute of Folk Heritage Library, Pakistan), [year dropped]" (pp. 211,
    228, folios not stated); "Qureshi, Samina. Lahore: The City Within. Singapore: Concept Media,
    [year dropped]" (p. 231, folio not stated); "Kanhyalal, Hindi. Tarikh-e Lahore. [year dropped].
    Reprint, Lahore: Majlis-e Tariqi-e Adab, [year dropped]" (pp. 193, 211, 226, folios not stated).
    **Every year in all three is destroyed.**
  - The author's own family is placed in the city: "the old walled city of Lahore from where I trace
    the ancestry of my maternal family" (p. 24, folio not stated); her ancestors "were appointed as
    Muftis (religious scholars) of the city of Lahore in the reign of the Muslim slave king Shamsuddin
    Iltutmish" (p. 25, folio not stated); "the Muftis and Gardezis who belonged to Lahore and Multan"
    (p. 26, folio not stated); "Mufti Ghulam Sarwar, one of my ancestors, is mentioned together with my
    paternal grandfather, Mufti Muzaffar Din, in Kanhyalal's history of Lahore. Mufti Ghulam Sarwar is
    the author of a history of the Panjab, Tarikh-e Makhazan Panjab" (p. 193, folio not stated).
  - Lahore is the city the author leaves from and returns to in her fieldwork: "Kasur is seventy miles
    from Lahore" (p. 86, folio not stated); she "started from Lahore on a beautiful spring afternoon"
    (p. 105, folio not stated); "Khalida and Jaleel Naqvi participated in the fieldwork in Lahore and
    Kasur" (p. 30, folio not stated); and two of them are shrine custodians elsewhere: "Naqvi, Jaleel,
    and Khalida Naqvi. Custodians, Hazrat Pir Sabir Sharif shrine, Paran Kalyar. Personal interviews
    and fieldwork partners. Lahore, –" — **the year range destroyed** (p. 217, folio not stated).
  - A grassroots musician is interviewed there: "Zarina, Khatoon. Grassroots musician. Personal
    interview. Lahore" (p. 218, folio not stated). Landlords' sons now "live in large urban centers
    like Karachi, Lahore, or Islamabad" (p. 81, folio not stated). Jhang Siyal is placed "to the
    southwest of Lahore in Pakistan" (pp. 121–122, folios not stated). Shalimar Gardens are "Gardens
    built by Shah Jehan, the Mughal emperor" (p. 188, folio not stated) — **no building date: (not
    stated)** — and the Melā Chirāg is held there annually (pp. 95, 185, folios not stated).
  - **`mazar-e-iqbal` reaches this list through Lahore, not through the book's four mentions of
    "Allama Iqbal Open University"** (pp. 204, 210, 217, 218, folios not stated), which name an
    institution and not the person; the book never names Iqbal as a man, an author or a literary
    reference anywhere in the eleven chunks.

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: the Bibi Pak Daman's arrival, "according to tradition", when the land was known as "Hind"; book's historical prose reporting tradition — "Supposedly", "It is reported that", "are said to have"): the Bibi Pak Daman "involved the men in disseminating Islam among the lay non-Muslim populations in the Panjab. The women spoke to the male scholars and to their audiences from behind the veil"; "seven hundred male scholars came with the Bibi Pak Daman to Hind, as the land was known then" (p. 161, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: period not stated, given in the context of Shah Inayat's descent in Bulle Shah's time; author's argument, and glossary definition): "The arāĩ in the Panjab were gardeners or small cultivators who worked on their own land" (p. 108, folio not stated); the Glossary defines instead "arāĩ A caste of landowners in the Panjab" (p. 178, folio not stated). The two definitions are kept side by side.
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: period not stated, present tense, in the discussion of Waris Shah's Hir; author's argument resting on endnoted sources, glossary definition): "The Jats of the Panjab are zamindars (landowners), known for practicing widow-marriage"; the Siyal tribe "is one of the few tribes in the Panjab that allow women to inherit property under customary law" (p. 121, folio not stated); the Glossary adds "Siyal Clan name of Hir's family; powerful landowners of the Panjab" (p. 188, folio not stated), "Kher ās" [text-layer?: Kheṛās] "A powerful clan of landowners in the Panjab mentioned in the Hir-Ranjha myth" (pp. 183–184, folios not stated), and, under satti, "Some sections of Indian society, such as the Siyal Jats of the Panjab, permitted a widow to remarry, and she was not forced into satti" (p. 189, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: Waris Shah's time, "the eighteenth century" as the book dates his Hir at p. 120, and "now"; author's argument): Waris Shah "is said to have recited it with great emotion among his speech communities in the Panjab"; "there is now substantial recorded material of Waris Shah's Hir available", and "Many renowned folk artists in the Panjab, such as Reshma, Zahida Parvin, Nazir Begum, Sabiha Khanum, Tufail Niazi, and Iqbal Bahu, have sung it" (p. 123, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Panjabi wedding", "Panjabi ritual"; refers to: "traditionally", period not stated; author's argument, endnote): Hir's lament as she is carried off in the palanquin "is traditionally sung at almost every Panjabi wedding at the time of the bride's departure to her husband's home" (p. 124, folio not stated); an endnote adds that "The ḍolī (palanquin) has a strong emotional context in Panjabi ritual, as a complex of emotions are associated with it" (p. 206, folio not stated). "Panjabi" here is a cultural adjective, not a place name.
- (book's word: "the Siraiki-speaking area of the Panjab", "the Panjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; author's argument, glossary definition): the Sohni myth "is sung in Sufi poetry in all the regions of the Sind, the Siraiki-speaking area of the Panjab" (pp. 126–127, folios not stated); the Glossary calls Mahival "The hero in the Sohni-Mahival myth of the Panjab" (p. 184, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; glossary definition): "dhammāl The vigorous ritual dance performed at Sufi shrines in Panjab and Sind" (p. 180, folio not stated).

    Siddiq (Lahore)", "Alaf Din Pathan (Lahore)" (p. 282, folio 262). **No shrine, no assembly, no

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Panjab", "Panjabi"; refers to: period not stated, ethnographic present of the author's fieldwork; author's argument): "tunes from the two principal regions of the Qawwali tradition, Uttar Pradesh and Panjab, can be distinguished mainly by their rhythmic setting"; "A predominantly quantitative emphasis characterizes tunes from Uttar Pradesh, while Panjabi tunes tend to be rhythmically organized according to 'quality' or stress"; the drum articulation of the metre, "which in Panjab is more strongly accentual than in Uttar Pradesh, corresponding with the respective drumming traditions of each region (as elaborated in Stewart 1974)" (p. 73, folio 53). The music example that follows is captioned "Panjab (Kisi ko kuchh)" against "Uttar Pradesh (Kachhjagmag)" (p. 75, folio 55), and the same song is said to exemplify "the 'Panjabi style'" (p. 58, folio 38). The book does not say whether its "Panjab" is the Pakistani or the Indian side, or both; it sets it against an Indian region (Uttar Pradesh), and the "Panjab" example was sung by "a Panjabi Qawwal, Rahmat Khan" (p. 57, folio 37) and recorded at "Qawwali Hall, 'Urs Nizamuddin" / "Auliya, 18 Apr. 1976" (p. 13, front matter, folio xi by the front-matter rule). Borderline: kept because it is a statement about the region's musical tradition in general, not a language remark.

*Sweep notes:* Hits in the `.txt` chunks for the brief's pattern (case-insensitive, widened to catch diacritic loss with `P[aue]n.?j.?[a-z]?b`): "Panjab" 3 (p. 73 ×2; p. 75 caption); "Panjabi" 6 (p. 13 CD listing "(Panjabi version) Panjabi Qawwal and Party" ×2; p. 57 "a Panjabi Qawwal, Rahmat Khan"; p. 58 "'Panjabi style'"; p. 73 "Panjabi tunes"; p. 256 glossary "ang     regional or genre style (for example, Panjabi ang)", folio 236 by rule); "Punjabi" 1 (bibliography title "A Punjabi Village in Pakistan", Eglar, date printed "i960" [text-layer?] [1960], p. 269); "Sind" 3, all bibliography titles (p. 267 BALOCH, "Musical Instruments of the Lower Indus Valley ofSind" [text-layer?] [of Sind]; p. 274 "'Music and Culture in Sind: An Ethnomusicological Perspective'" and "Sind through the Centuries"); "Pathan" 1 (performer name "Alaf Din Pathan (Lahore)", discography, p. 282); "Punjab"/"Sindh"/"Baluchistan"/"Balochistan"/"Frontier"/"Pakhtunkhwa"/"NWFP"/"Kashmir"/"Multan"/"suba"/"Sindhi"/"Baluchi"/"Kashmiri": 0. The case-insensitive "Sind" pattern also matched "simultaneous(ly)" 11 times (chunks 006, 008, 012, 013): false positives. Excluded: the p. 13 CD listing, the p. 57 performer description and the p. 256 glossary entry (person or terminology, folded into the bullet as context only where they bear on it); all bibliography and discography lines (titles and a name, no substantive text). No toponym-only province hits. Flags for a human: the single bullet's "Panjab" is unbounded in the book (no India/Pakistan side stated) and its illustrative recording was made at the ʿurs of Nizamuddin Auliya, which the takeaways place in Delhi — a reviewer may prefer to move it to `not attached`.

## From Robert Rozehnal, Islamic Sufism Unbound

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/rozehnal_islamic_sufism_unbound.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

One sentence, which supplies the setting for a miracle story.

- **The whole evidence.** "Once Captain Wahid Bakhsh Sahab and Hazrat Shahidullah Sahab went to
  visit the shrine of the saint Miyan Mir in Lahore" (p. 72, folio 62), narrated to the author by
  "another senior male murid" (May 2, 2001, Bahawalpur). **The book's spelling is `Miyan Mir`,
  against the archive's `Mian Mir`** — recorded so it is not read as a different figure.
- **What happened there.** A majhzub at the shrine asked Shahidullah his hometown; he answered "My
  hometown is London"; the majhzub laughed and said "No, no. Your hometown is Medina!" and "repeated
  this many times" (p. 72, folio 62).
- **Nothing else.** No death date, no silsila, no street location, no second name, no ʿurs, no
  custodian, no architecture: all **(not stated)**.

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **`shrine-of-mian-mir` rests on one sentence** (p. 72, folio 62), which supplies a setting for a
  majhzub anecdote and nothing else. The book's spelling is `Miyan Mir`.

### Place-level material: Lahore

*The book's place-level material on "Lahore" (35 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** (the book's "Lahore", 35 rows — the heaviest attachment in this book, as the ruling intends):
  - **Lahore is the book's base.** "I spent the academic year of 1995–1996 as a graduate student
    studying Urdu in Lahore, Pakistan" (p. 21, folio 10); "In Pakistan, I was based in the cultural
    capital of Lahore" (p. 26, folio 15) — **"the cultural capital" is the book's own epithet for the
    city.** It is also the city where the photograph of the dead shaykh was circulated among
    "several friends in Lahore" (p. 21, folio 10).
  - **The city as the order's densest living node.** "frequent attendance at the zikr circles in
    Lahore" is part of the author's fieldwork (p. 141, folio 133). Two weekly halqa-i zikr run there,
    both in private homes and one of them women-led: "a senior male murid hosts a weekly halqa-i zikr
    in his home," and "A female disciple of Shaykh Wahid Bakhsh Rabbani does the same, leading a
    separate session comprised mostly of women" (p. 201, folio 193). The full eleven-item zikr
    repertoire the book prints was recorded "at a gathering on a Thursday evening in October 2000"
    in Lahore (p. 202, folio 194). **Hospitality is banned at these gatherings:** "That is why
    serving any food or even a cup of tea is strictly forbidden, because some people then might come
    just to socialize" (August 26, 2001, Lahore) (p. 203, folio 195) — **there is no langar.**
  - **Lahore as the order's print capital.** The Sufi Foundation, "founded by another senior male
    murid, published a series of translations and original writings by Wahid Bakhsh during the
    1980s"; Bazm-i Ittehad al-Muslimin was "founded in 1984 by the shaykh's nephew"; "Beginning in
    1996, Talifat-i Shaheedi, established by another murid of Shahidullah Faridi, has published four
    additional texts, three of them in English" (p. 130, folio 121). Commercial houses used: "Ziya
    al-Qur'an… currently publishes two of Wahid Bakhsh's Urdu translations of classical Persian
    malfuzat; Qausin Publishers prints two key Urdu malfuzat; and, most importantly, al-Faisal
    Publishers distributes a number of Wahid Bakhsh's works in both English and Urdu" (p. 130,
    folio 121). Also named: Sh. Muhammad Ashraf (p. 270, folio 264), Vanguard Books
    (p. 241, folio 234) and `al-Kitab` (p. 263, folio not stated) — and the silsila's books reach the
    public through "the Urdu bookstores of Karachi and Lahore" (p. 111, folio 102). Across all
    houses, "At present, some fifty different texts are produced and distributed from a number of
    publishing houses" (p. 130, folio 121).
  - **A women's madrasa.** A murid "now runs an informal religious school (madrasa) for women in
    Lahore" (p. 44, folio 34).
  - **Lahore as a seat of state and press.** "the governor of Punjab in Lahore" (p. 39, folio 29);
    "the Lahore-based English language weekly, The Friday Times" (p. 37, folio 27); the *Journal of
    the Panjab Historical Society (Lahore)* (p. 242, folio 235; p. 270, folio 264).
  - **Lahore as a distance benchmark for another shrine:** Pakpattan is "120 miles southwest of
    Lahore" (p. 35, folio 25).
  - **A death and a funeral in the city.** Faruq Ahmad "died in Lahore on February 13, 1945 after a
    prolonged bout of pneumonia" (p. 75, folio 65); "Throughout the time of the funeral, a light rain
    fell from that cloud of mercy only above that spot in Lahore" (p. 75, folio 65).
  - **The city's shrines as a class, in the order's own words.** "There are many mazars of unknown
    shaykhs in Lahore. Some of them are qalandars [a roaming sect of antinomian Sufis] and can only
    give their visitors what they have—that is, they make them qalandars as well. In our silsila we
    avoid this." (p. 207, folio 199) **This is a statement about Lahore's shrines in general and
    names none of them; it is the clearest case in the book for the place-level reading.**
  - **Interview datelines, recorded because the ruling attaches them and because they date the
    ethnography**: March 9, 2001; February 21, 2001; October 1, 2001; April 24, 2001 (pp. 44, 46, 47,
    folios 34, 36, 37); October 3, 2000; November 5, 2000; June 17, 2001; August 26, 2001 (pp. 91,
    94–96, 131, folios 81, 84–86, 122); October 22, 2000; March 16, 2001; February 26, 2001 (pp. 167,
    169, 173, folios 159, 161, 165); October 30 and November 7, 2000; April 25, 2001; October 7, 2000
    (pp. 188, 191, 193, 225, folios 180, 183, 185, 217); October 31, 2000 (p. 208, folio 200);
    October 3, 2002 (p. 132, folio 123).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: British colonial rule — the book's frame is "the British Raj", no years given in the sentence; quoted source: Katherine Ewing, *Arguing Sainthood*, 49, per n. 10): "British policy, especially in the Punjab and Sind, was to treat them in essentially the same way that landlords and tribal leaders were treated . . . They tried to maintain the traditional social structure intact, securing the loyalty of the pirs, landlords, and chiefs by reinforcing their economic positions and educating them in the British tradition." — "them" being "The hereditary saints [sajjada nishin]", who "were seen primarily in terms of their economic power" (p. 33, folio 23). Note 10 points for "the British policy toward local Sufi institutions and exemplars in the Punjab" to Ansari's *Sufi Saints and State Power: The Pirs of Sind, 1843–1947* and Gilmartin's *Empire and Islam: Punjab and the Making of Pakistan* (p. 241, folio 234; bibliographic only).
- (book's word: "the southern Punjab"; refers to: from Wahid Bakhsh's khilafat being "publicly confirmed" "In 1975" and Shahidullah Faridi's "last will and testament", no year printed for the will; author's argument): Shahidullah's will says Wahid Bakhsh "has initiated halqa zikr and guidance in Bahawalpur" and "should spread the silsila in this place only"; the author reads this: "Here Shahidullah effectively delineates the southern Punjab as Wahid Bakhsh’s personal spiritual domain (wilayat)", adding that "In the following years, however, the shaykh’s duties Chishti Sabiri master rapidly expanded in scope and scale" (sentence printed so, a word apparently missing; not repaired) (p. 90, folio 80). The will names only Bahawalpur; the extension to "the southern Punjab" is the author's.

## From Annemarie Schimmel, As Through a Veil

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/schimmel_as_through_a_veil.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Place-level material: Lahore

*The book's material headed "Lahore (place-level: 35 rows)", copied into every existing entry that heading lists (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

**Kind of evidence: OVERWHELMINGLY IMPRINT-ONLY — a place of publication or a publisher's address in
the bibliography and the notes, not the book discussing Lahore.** Chunk 014 states it flatly: "in this
chunk every single 'Lahore' is a place of publication or a publisher's address — the book nowhere
discusses Lahore itself here." Chunk 010 says the toponym "occurs twice, both times in one note, and
both times only as a place of publication," and calls the attachment "place-level and weaker than
place-level." **Chunk 009's entire Lahore attachment — all 35 rows — rests on the single imprint
"(Lahore, 1932)".** **Exactly two facts below are genuinely topical** and are the only Lahore material
about the city itself: Hujwiri's last years in Lahore (p. 61, folio 51) and Madho Lal Husain's tomb
near the Shalimar gardens (pp. 170–171, folios 152–153). A shrine-entry writer can drop everything
else in this subsection as bibliographic apparatus.

**Topical (the book on Lahore itself) — two facts only**
- Lahore is named once in twenty-five pages of chunk 003, and only as the place where a book was
  finished: "The `Kash/ al-mafJjub`[text-layer?: Kashf al-maḥjūb] of Hujwiri, who spent his last years
  in Lahore, is the first major treatise on Sufi theories in Persian" (p. 61, folio 51).
- Madho Lal Husain "is buried together with his Hindu beloved in a modest tomb near the Shalimar
  gardens in Lahore" (pp. 170–171, folios 152–153). This is chunk 007's only Lahore occurrence. The
  Shalimar gardens are named only as the landmark for that tomb; nothing else is said about them.
- Negative finding (chunk 003): no shrine, tomb, mosque, ʿurs, custodian, endowment or building in
  Lahore is mentioned, and the city is not otherwise described (not stated) (p. 61, folio 51 context).

**Index**
- `Lahore` stands as its own headword in the Index of Proper Names (p. 357, folio 339).

**Imprint-only — notes**
- Iqbal's "epic ]avidniima (Lahore, 1932), lines 727-728", note 2 to chapter 1, printed on p. 233
  (folio not stated). This single imprint is the whole of chunk 009's Lahore evidence; the chunk says
  nothing else about the city, and nothing at all about any Lahore site, saint, shrine or practice.
- "Part translation in verse by Pir Imam Shah, Lahore 191 1"[text-layer?: 1911], "fly leaf. Another
  Panjabi verse translation by Maulana Muhammad Shahuddin, Lahore, 1939 (see L. D. Barnett, Panjabi,
  p. 39)." (p. 254, folio 236). The note is note 3 of the Rumi chapter and stands two notes after "The
  literature about Jalaluddin is almost inexhaustible"; **the work being translated is not named in the
  note itself** (not stated). So chunk 010's whole Lahore holding is: two Panjabi verse translations
  were published at Lahore, one in "191 1"[text-layer?: 1911] and one in "1939", by "Pir Imam Shah" and
  "Maulana Muhammad Shahuddin" respectively (p. 254, folio 236).
- Note 77: "A fine Panjabi version with a commentary by Haji Muhammad ad­din Naqshbandi Mujaddidi from
  Bombay was published in the `Alliihwiile ki qaumi dukkiin` [text-layer?: Allāhwāle kī qaumī dukkān],
  Lahore; it preserves the rhyme letter m, followed by the genitive particle di." (p. 301, folio 283).

**Imprint-only — bibliography**
- The same Lahore imprint as note 77, under a different spelling: "' Abd al-' Ali `I;Iafi�`
  [text-layer?: Ḥāfiẓ] Jhanda. Guldasta-i `l;:lafiz;` [text-layer?: Ḥāfiẓ] Jhanda. Lahore: Allah-wale
  kr qaumi dukan, n . d ." (p. 311, folio not stated).
- "c Abdullah, Dr. Syed . Naqd-i Mir. Lahore: Ayina-i adab, 1958 ." (p. 311, folio not stated).
- Two Lahore reprints by the same publisher: Affifi, The Mystical Philosophy of `Mul1yid'Din`
  [text-layer?: Muḥyīʾd-Dīn] Ibnul-cArabi, "Cambridge: Cambridge University Press, 1936; repr. Lahore:
  Ashraf, n.d." and "Ahmad, Zubaid . The Contribution of Indo-Pakistan to Arabic Literature. 2d ed .
  Lahore: Ashraf, 1968 ." (p. 312, folio 294).
- "Bu c Alf Qalandar. Diwan. Lahore, n.d." (p. 316, folio 298) — a Lahore edition with neither
  publisher nor year given.
- "Lahore: Majlis-i ta-raqqi-e Urdu, 196i [text-layer?: 1961?]" — publisher of "Urda Diwan
  [text-layer?: Urdū Dīwān], ed. Khalil ur-Ra):i.man Da'udi [text-layer?: Khalīl ur-Raḥmān Dāʾūdī]"
  (p. 318, folio 300).
- "repr. Lahore: Ashraf, 1952" — reprint of "Gairdner, W. H . Temple. Al-Ghazzalf's
  [text-layer?: Al-Ghazzālī's] 'Mishkat al-anwar': The Niche for Lights" (p. 321, folio 303).
- "Lahore: University of the Punjab, 1969" — twice, for "Ghalib, Mirza Asadullah . Kulliyiit-i fiirsf
  [text-layer?: Kulliyāt-i fārsī]. Persian works in 17 vols." and for "Urdii Diwan
  [text-layer?: Urdū Dīwān], ed . l:famid c Ali Khan [text-layer?: Ḥāmid ʿAlī Khān]" (p. 321, folio 303).
- "Lahore: Allah wale ki qaumi dukan, s . d . No. 200" — "Ghulam Jrlani Qadirr, Pir Sayyid
  [text-layer?: Ghulām Jīlānī Qādirī]. Prem piyiila [text-layer?: Prem piyāla]" (p. 321, folio 303).
- "Lahore: Ashraf, 1930 and often" and eight further "Lahore: n.p." imprints for Iqbal
  (pp. 325–326, folios 307–308).
- "Lahore: University of the Punjab, 1955" — the "Muhammad Shafic Presentation Volume, ed. Dr. Syed
  Abdullah" (p. 326, folio 308). *(This is the same volume in which Ivanow's "Shums Tabrez of Multan"
  appeared — see Multan below.)*
- "Lahore: Ashraf, 1956" — "Iqbal aur cashq-i rasiil" (p. 326, folio 308).
- "Lahore: Allah wale ki qaumi dukan, n.d. No. 195" — "Majmaca Panj ganj
  [text-layer?: Majmūʿa Panj ganj] (Panjabi Mystical Verse)", an entry with **no author at all**
  (p. 329, folio 311).
- "Lahore: Urdu Dunya, 1958" — "Mir Taqf Mir [text-layer?: Mīr Taqī Mīr]. Kulliyat, ed. Dr. Ebadet
  Brelwi" (p. 331, folio 313).
- "Lahore: Allahwale ki qaumi dukan, n.d. Nr. 87" — "Mul;lammad ad-din, I:Iajjf Maulana
  [text-layer?: Muḥammad ad-Dīn, Ḥājjī Maulānā]. Qas.ida-i Burda Panjabi" (p. 331, folio 313).
- "Lahore: Allahwale ki qaumi dukiin, n . d . , no. 372" — the Bullhe Shah commentary
  (p. 333, folio 315).
- "2d ed. Lahore: Minerva Bookshop, 1947" — "Smith, Wilfred Cantwell . Islam in Modern India"
  (p. 340, folio 322).
- "Lahore: Ashraf, 1967" — "Sultan Bahoo. Abyat" (p. 341, folio 323).
- "Waheed Mirza . The Life and Works of Amir Khusrau. 3d ed. Lahore: University of the Punjab, 1962;
  repr. 1975." (p. 343, folio 325).
- "Tu/ifa Ra/iim Yar. Lahore: Allahwale k i qaumi dukan, n . d . ( c . 1951)."
  [text-layer?: `Tuḥfa Raḥīm Yār`] (p. 342, folio 324).

**Lahore publishers and institutions, as printed**
- "Ashraf", "Allah wale ki qaumi dukan" / "Allahwale ki qaumi dukan" (a numbered chapbook series —
  "No. 200", "No. 195", "Nr. 87", "no. 372"), "University of the Punjab", "Majlis-i ta-raqqi-e Urdu",
  "Urdu Dunya", "Minerva Bookshop" (pp. 318, 321, 325, 326, 329, 331, 333, 340, 341).

**Contradiction kept side by side (not reconciled)**
- Chunks 010, 013 and 014 attach imprint lines as Lahore material under ruling (a). Chunk 015 says of
  the same kind of line: "Imprint lines are refused as place-level material — see `## Doubts`"
  (pp. 342–343, folios 324–325), while still heading its bullet with all 35 ids. The two positions are
  left standing as the chunks left them.

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "in the centuries after his death", i.e. after the historian Bada'uni, as the book states, with no dates given; book's historical prose): "only in the centuries after his death did mystical poets in Sind and the Punjab develop the art of utilizing simple folk tales as media for mystical instruction" (p. 154, folio 136).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated, the poets being "contemporaries" of "the urban poets in India"; book's historical prose): "While the urban poets in India expressed their veneration of the Prophet in grandiose hymns to display their erudition, their contemporaries in the plains of Sind and the Punjab wrote tender little poems or instructive Golden Alphabets to instil love for him into the souls of the villagers" (p. 222, folio 204).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument, endnote 80 to "Mystical Poetry in the Vernaculars"): the story of Muhammad ibn al-Hanafiyya, "a son of 'Ali by another wife than Fatima", "is found in the Panjab, in Bengal, South India, and Malaysia" (p. 284, folio 266).
- (book's word: "Panjabis"; refers to: present tense, no date; the version is dated "the late eighteenth century"; author's argument): the romance of Hir Ranjha (printed "Hfr Riinjhii" [text-layer?] [Hīr Rānjhā]), "elaborated in more than a hundred versions in Panjabi, Urdu, Sindhi, and Persian, was given its final form in the late eighteenth century by Warith Shah. It is his verse which is sung, time and again, wherever Panjabis meet" (p. 171, folio 153). This is about a people, not a territory, and the book does not say where they meet.

## From Annemarie Schimmel, Mystical Dimensions of Islam

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/schimmel_mystical_dimensions_of_islam.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

Printed `Mian Mir` throughout (pp. 392, 394, 464, 521). **The book never uses the archive's form
`Mir Mohammad of Sind`** — but it independently supplies the Sind origin.

- **Saint**
  - **Death year, agreeing with the archive**: the index prints `Mian Mir (d. 1635), 361, 433`
    (p. 521, folio `49°` [text-layer?: 490]). The running text gives him **no** date — `(not stated)`
    at pp. 392, 394, 464.
  - Origin, which independently matches the row's own parenthesis: `the Qadiriyya, represented in
    Lahore by Mian Mir, who had come there from Sind, together with his mystically inclined sister
    Bibi Jamal` (p. 392, folio 361).
  - Kinship: `The sister of Jihanara's and Dara's first mystical guide Mian Mir, Bibi Jamal Khatun
    (d. 1639), was one of the outstanding saints of the Qadiriyya order during its formative period in
    the Punjab.` (p. 464, folio 433). **His order is given only by implication, through his sister's
    Qadiriyya** — (not stated) in his own right.
  - He is `the first mystical guide` of `Shah Jihan's eldest daughter Jihanara` and of `her luckless
    brother Dara Shikoh` (p. 464, folio 433).
  - **No tomb, no shrine, no Lahore quarter, no Alam Ganj, no Dharampura anywhere** — `(not stated)`
    (pp. 392, 394, 464, 521).
- **Significance**
  - *The Mogul heir apparent's shaykh, at one remove*: `Mian Mir's disciple and successor, Molla Shah
    Badakhshi, introduced the prince to the saint.` (p. 392, folio 361).
  - *A seventeenth-century Qadiri biography of Lahore exists, written by the Mogul heir apparent*:
    Dara Shikoh wrote `a fine biography of Mian Mir and the Qadiriyya in Lahore`, the `Sakinat
    al-auliyff` [text-layer?: Sakīnat al-auliyāʾ] (p. 392, folio 361). The book gives the work no date
    — (not stated).
  - *The succession*: `Her master Molla Shah thought her worthy to be his successor, though this did
    not materialize` — of Jihanara, Dara's and Aurangzeb's eldest sister (p. 394, folio 363).

### Place-level material: Lahore

*The book's place-level material on "Lahore" (35 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** (the book's `Lahore` — all 35 rows in that place, ruling (a)): the city is given four
  substantive statements in the whole book, and one more inside an article title.
  - **The founding statement, at the moment of the Ghaznawid conquest**: `Lahore became the first
    center of Persian-inspired Muslim culture in the Subcontinent—the name of Hujwiri, who composed
    his famous Persian treatise on Sufism in this town, has already been mentioned; his tomb still
    provides a place of pilgrimage for the Panjabis.` (p. 376, folio 345). Its setting: `The second
    wave of Muslim conquest in India, that of the Ghaznawids about the year 1000` [text-layer?:
    clean]`, brought into the Subcontinent not only scholars like al-Blruni [text-layer?: al-Bīrūnī]
    (d. 1048 [text-layer?: clean]), who made a careful study of Hindu philosophy and life, but
    theologians and poets as well.` (p. 376, folio 345).
  - **Political status**: `Lahore, the capital of the Ghaznawids in India` (p. 119, folio 88), where
    Hujwīrī `died there in 1071` and where `His shrine, called that of Data Ganj Bakhsh, is still a
    popular place of pilgrimage in Lahore.` (p. 119, folio 88).
  - **The seat of the Qadiriyya in Mogul India, and the order is said to have arrived there from
    Sind**: `It was not the "official" Chishti order to which he was attracted, but rather the
    Qadiriyya, represented in Lahore by Mian Mir, who had come there from Sind, together with his
    mystically inclined sister Bibi Jamal.` (p. 392, folio 361); and a seventeenth-century Qadiri
    biography *of Lahore* exists, written by the Mogul heir apparent — `The Sakinat al-auliyff
    [text-layer?: Sakīnat al-auliyāʾ] is a fine biography of Mian Mir and the Qadiriyya in Lahore.`
    (p. 392, folio 361). The book gives that work no date — (not stated).
  - **An annual festival, fixed by place, date and name**: `Madho Lai's anniversary, still celebrated
    in Lahore near the Shalimar Garden on the last Saturday in March, is famous as Meld Chiraghdn
    [text-layer?: Melā Chirāghān], the "Fair of Lamps."` (p. 415, folio 384). No attendance figure,
    no duration, no custodian, no founding year — all (not stated).
  - **A book trade**: of al-Būṣīrī's *Burda*, `Copies are still being sold in the shops of Lahore and
    elsewhere.` (p. 256, folio 225). No shop, no bazaar, no date for the observation — (not stated).
  - **A burial**: `a few years ago a maqam of Muhammad Iqbal (who is buried in Lahore) was erected in
    the garden of Jalaluddin Ruml's tomb in Konya` (p. 270, folio 239). `a few years ago` is the
    book's own dating and no year is given — (not stated).
  - **Residence by proximity only**: `Bullhe Shah (1680-1752) … lived near Lahore.` (p. 419,
    folio 388). The book does not say where he is buried and does not name Kasur — (not stated).
  - **Inside an article title, and fired by chunk 026 under ruling (a) because §6(ii) exempts only
    imprints and index locators**: `Massignon, Louis, and Huart, Clemens. "Les entretiens de Lahore."`
    / `Journal asiatique 209 (1926).` (p. 486, folio 455; the same article cited at p. 391, folio 360,
    footnote 23). **The entire content is the entry as printed.** The Pass-1 worker recorded being
    uncomfortable with this firing — see `## REVIEW (worker A)`.
  - **Nothing else in the book is said of Lahore as a place.** No mosque, no other shrine, no quarter,
    no building date, no modern condition — **(not stated)**. Every other `Lahore` in the book (some
    fifty occurrences across chunks 007, 009, 013, 014, 015, 016, 017, 018, 019, 021, 022, 023, 025,
    026) is **a publisher's imprint city in a footnote or a bibliography entry**, or the index entry
    `Lahore, 88, 225, 239, 345, 361, 384, 388` (p. 520, folio 489), and all of those were declined.

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: the Mogul period, "the Mogul emperors (especially Akbar)"; author's argument): interest in Rumi's work "seems to have been common to all classes of society—the Mogul emperors (especially Akbar) were as fond of him as the simple villagers in Sind and Punjab" (p. 358, folio 327).
- (book's word: "Punjabi"; refers to: present day ("still claim"), looking back to "Nizamuddln's time" [text-layer?: Niẓāmuddīn's]; ethnographic present): in Nizamuddin's time "Sufism became a mass movement in northwestern India", and "A number of Punjabi tribes still claim to have been converted by Fariduddin Ganj-i Shakar" (p. 381, folio 350; header printed `35O` [text-layer?: 350]).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: the time of "Shah Ismacll [text-layer?: Ismāʿīl] Shahid" and "Sayyid Ahmad of Bareilly", no date given for the occupation; book's historical prose): both were "known for their brave fight against the Sikh, who had occupied the whole of the Punjab and part of the northwestern frontier" (p. 403, folio 372). The book's unit here is the Punjab as occupied by "the Sikh"; carried as printed.
- (book's word: "a Panjabi village", "The Punjab"; refers to: present day, with Warith Shah's form dated "in the late eighteenth century"; ethnographic present and author's argument): "The visitor who stays in a Panjabi village will certainly find somebody to sing for him the old romance of Hir Ranjha in the classical form given it by Warith Shah"; "The Punjab and the Indus valley have a common tradition of mystical folk literature" (p. 414, folio 383; the section heading `MYSTICAL POETRY IN THE REGIONAL LANGUAGES— SINDHI, PANJABI, PASHTO` begins mid-page under an ordinary header).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "the two provinces"; refers to: "back to the fifteenth century", with Madho Lal Husayn as "sixteenth-century"; book's historical prose): "Reliable tradition traces the use of the native language for mystical poetry both in Sind and in the Punjab back to the fifteenth century"; Madho Lal's allusions to the stories of Hir and of Sohni Mehanwal "seems to inaugurate (or perhaps even continue from unknown beginnings) a tradition typical of the religious poetry of the two provinces: the insertion of folk tales into the context of mystical thought" (p. 415, folio 384).
- (book's word: "The Punjab"; refers to: period not stated (an eighteenth-century Panjabi poet is quoted); author's argument): "The Punjab and Sind show close similarities in the types of mystical literature that nourished there"; Panjabi and Sindhi "were excellent media to express mystical feelings, though not mystical theories"; the themes "were sung time and again in forms inherited from the Indian past—dhoras, kdfh [text-layer?: kāfī], way—not in the Persian form of ghazal", in forms "closely connected with indigenous musical modes"; the attack on the "molld" [text-layer?: mollā] was taken up "by the mystics in the northwest part of the Subcontinent", and such verses "appealed particularly to the peasants and the illiterate" (p. 416, folio 385).
- (book's word: "both provinces"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): "In both provinces, a preference for the motif of spinning and weaving can be observed, a natural propensity in a cotton-growing country"; "Both provinces have also produced hymns extolling the great masters of the mystical orders, particularly cAbduDl-Qadir Gilanl [text-layer?: ʿAbduʾl-Qādir Gīlānī]"; the imagery "could easily be understood by everybody in the village" (p. 417, folio 386).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated, the example Sheikh Tahir is "sixteenth-century"; author's argument): "A strange fact—more visible in Sind than in the Punjab—is the way Hindus shared in the Muslim orders. Some saints were claimed by both communities" (p. 418, folio 387).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "after 1800" and "the twentieth century"; book's historical prose): "In the Punjab, the literary heritage of Panjabi was rarely continued by Muslims after 1800, though in the twentieth century a number of mystical treatises have been written and published in Panjabi, including a part translation of Rumi's Mathnawi" (printed "Rumi'sMathnawi"); the language "was used almost exclusively by the Sikhs as their literary medium, whereas the Panjabi Muslims resorted to Urdu", "though they enjoyed and still enjoy listening to the simple mystical verses and ballads in their spoken mother tongue" (p. 427, folio 396; header printed `306` [text-layer?: 396]).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument, Appendix 1): the Ahmad bila mim tradition "has been repeated in high literature as much as in popular mystical poetry, be it among the Turkish Bektashis or among the mystical bards of Sind and the Punjab" (p. 451, folio 420; header printed `42O` [text-layer?: 420]).
- (book's word: "Punjab", "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated, Sayyid Muhammad Jiw Jan is "in the seventeenth century"; author's argument, Appendix 2): "In the province of Sind, as well as in Punjab, a special aspect of mystical appreciation of women can be observed that is rarely encountered in other parts of the Muslim world, i.e., the representation of the longing soul in the form of a woman"; the bridal symbolism "is used mostly in the mystically interpreted folk tales of Sind and the Punjab"; "The tragic heroines of the Sindhi and Panjabi folk tales—like Hir, Sassui, Sohni, and many others—represent the human soul in search of the beloved" (p. 465, folio 434).

## From Haroon Khalid, Walking with Nanak

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/khalid_walking_with_nanak.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

- **Saint**
  - "It is believed that the Guru [Hargobind] was released after the Muslim saint Mian Mir, who was also a friend of Guru Arjan, interceded on behalf of Guru Hargobind" (p. 235, folio 209).
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - In the Sikh version, "his friend, the Muslim saint of Lahore, Mian Mir, came to him and asked for permission to bury alive his oppressors. The Guru refused" (p. 253, folio 227).

### Place-level material: Lahore

*The book's material headed "Lahore (place-level: 35 rows)", copied into every existing entry that heading lists (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

Mostly thin and incidental. "The Lahore High Court" is treated as an institution, not the Baba Shah Chiragh site; the bibliography imprint "Lahore: Punjabi History Board" is not material.

- **Shrine / Darbar** (the city's religious history as the book gives it)
  - "In Lahore, I found that Muslims and Christians, whose ancestors were once Hindus, continue to visit a Hindu temple their ancestors visited" (temple not named) (p. 32, folio 14).
  - Iqbal Qaiser's LUMS lecture "was titled SikA [OCR?] [Sikh] Architecture in Lahore": "like the Sikh religion, which picks up from both Hinduism and Islam, Sikh architecture draws inspiration from both Hindu and Muslim architecture"; domes "initially part of the Muslim buildings" were made "smaller" by Sikhs, and "Now such domes are also used in Muslim buildings in Pakistan"; Sikh shrines carry "graphic depictions of Sikh Gurus and also sometimes Hindu deities (in older buildings)" (pp. 309–310, folio not stated / folio 284).
  - Rohtas "was not the kind of fort that was found in Lahore, where only royalty stayed" (p. 249, folio 223). Today "the mighty Ravi of history flows like a stream in Lahore" (damaged) (p. 281, folio 255).
  - "The Kashmiri binders who used to reside in Lahore were known for their meticulous binding" (p. 39, folio 21); Bhai Ram Singh was Principal of "Mayo School of Arts in Lahore" (p. 117, folio 99).
- **Practices & Events** (history the book sets in Lahore)
  - Babur, attacking Punjab, destroyed "the cities of Eminabad and Lahore on the way" (p. 15, folio not stated).
  - "the Governor of Lahore, Moin-ul-Mulk also known as Meer Manu" ordered the killing of Sikhs; "The Lahore High Court" heard the Shaheed Ganj case in the British era and "Around 1957-58" (p. 77, folio 59; p. 78, folio 60).
  - Guru Arjan "was summoned to Lahore by Emperor Jahangir" (p. 234, folio 208) and died there (haveli of the Mughal Diwan near the Rang Mahal; samadhi near Lahore Fort; the Ravi — see the Arjan group) (p. 253, folio 227); Chandu Lal was "appointed as the Diwan of Lahore by the Mughal authorities" (p. 253, folio 227); Sikh tradition traces the Prithi Chand–Arjan conflict to "Arja[n]'s trip to Lahore" (p. 274, folio 248); Khusro "rebelled against his father and moved towards Lahore" (p. 255, folio 229).
  - After defeat by Aurangzeb, Dara Shikoh "decided to head west to Lahore and continue his fight"; Guru Har Rai "along with his forces, reached Lahore" and met him there; Dara "had planned to flee to Multan from Lahore"; a version has Aurangzeb follow "after he fled to Lahore" (pp. 210–211, folios 184–185).
  - "In 1931" Bhagat Singh, Rajguru and Sukhdev "were hung at Lahore jail" (p. 48, folio 30).
  - Ghulab Singh is "the first Sikh police officer in the revamped traffic police of Lahore" (p. 35, folio 17); new apartments "built recently to cater to the growing demands of urban Lahore" (p. 36, folio 18); indigenous trees "now being replaced by imported ones in the developed areas of Lahore" (p. 46, folio 28); Bedian was founded "near the city of Lahore"; "The Bedian road is the old route to Amritsar", and Prithi Chand's rival shrine would lie "on the route from Lahore to Amritsar" (p. 38, folio 20; p. 44, folio 26).
  - Lahore is listed among cities whose Sikh communities have "Only recently" been able "to exert themselves" (p. 88, folio 70); Jasdeep studies at "Punjab University, Leh i) [OCR?] [Lahore?]" (p. 88, folio 70).
